18:                                                    The evaluation of the determinants is via a normal form for sums of products
19:                                                    of sines, giving a powerful system of trigonometric identities. The particular
20:                                                    result is generalized to arrangements derived from three edge connected totally
21:                                                    cyclic directed graphs, conjectured to be sufficient for a complete analysis of
22:                                                    angle constraining arrangements of lines, and thus a full response to Ringel’s
23:                                                    slope conjecture. These methods are generally applicable to the realizability
24:                                                    problem for rank 3 oriented matroids.

29:                                                                                 1. Introduction
30:                                               A more accurate, but less snappy, title for this paper might have been: “New
31:                                           Approaches to Angles and Arrangements of Lines and Pseudolines applied to Pap-
32:                                           pus’s Theorem”. This paper does contain a new proof of Pappus’s theorem, but
33:                                           it is fairly laborious and ugly. In particular, it is strangely asymmetric given the
161: these introduces the notion of a twisted graph, generalizing the line arrangement
162: studied in the main theorem. Section 15 shows that results similar to the main
163: theorem can be derived for many twisted graphs, including all totally cyclic, simply
164: three edge connected, directed cubic graphs. Conjectures of stronger results are
165: made.
166:    The final discussion, in section 16, concerns how these results may be relevant to
167: the pseudoline stretching problem, giving (without proof), a cryptomorphic axiom
168: system for rank 3 acyclic oriented matroids, suited for studying partial line arrange-
169: ments in the Euclidean plane. We suggest that every unrealizable rank 3 oriented
170: matroid contains a line arrangement that is a counterexample to Ringel’s slope con-
171: jecture, thus showing that the proof technique used to prove the unstretchability of
172: the non-Pappus arrangement is general. We discuss future directions for this work.
173:    The paper closes with a brief conclusion.
174: 
175:                         3. Context and Related Work
176: 3.1. Euclidean Geometry. Euclidean geometry is the oldest area of mathemat-
177: ical study, but is nowadays seen as essentially completed and not an area for
178: research. One of the claims of this paper is that angles have not been studied
179: adequately independently of the metric in Euclidean geometry (unlike the metric
180: independently of angles). As well as the angular variant of Ceva’s theorem, there
236: 
237: The main result of this paper is equivalent. The invaluable standard reference for
238: oriented matroids is [Björner et al., 1999].
239: 3.4. Ringel’s Slope Conjecture. [Ringel, 1956] conjectured that in an arrange-
240: ment of lines in general position the slopes could be arbitrarily prescribed. This
241: conjecture was disproved first by [Las Vergnas, 1986] using oriented matroid tech-
242: niques over a 32 point dual construction. [Richter-Gebert and Sturmfels, 1991] im-
243: proved this to give a 6 line counterexample (fig. 2(c)), still demonstrating the slope
244: constraint using oriented matroid techniques. [Felsner and Ziegler, 2001] give a
245: different proof of the counterexample using higher Bruhat orders. [Carroll, 2006]
246: demonstrates the result using schoolbook geometry. The main theorem of this pa-
247: per is another counter-example. The theme of the more general analysis of this
248: paper, sections 13 to 16, is the search for all minimal counter-examples to this
249: conjecture.
250: 3.5. Venn Triangles. This paper builds on my earlier work1 which suffers from
251: complete ignorance of the field. This was motivated by a specific pseudoline stretch-
252: ing problem, relating to drawing diagrams of 6-Venn triangles [Carroll, 2000c].
253: While I produced a pseudoline stretching program that stretched the diagrams
254: of interest to me at that time, I could not adequately explain why it worked. This
255: paper is a move towards an explanation. The key insight of my earlier work, that
256: is not in the literature, is that the use of polar coordinates allow the pseudoline
257: stretching problem to be divided into two separate phases: first determine the θ co-
258: ordinates of the lines, and second, use linear programming techniques to determine
259: the r coordinates. The latter step is a solved problem, although issues are presented
705: 8.2. Relationship with Pappus.
706: Proof of Pappus’s theorem 8.3. Let N be the point of intersection of the two lines.
707:    If N, A, B, C, D, E and F are not all distinct, then the theorem is trivial.
708:    Take a counterexample to the theorem.
709:    By relabelling we can assume that A and C are both adjacent to N on one line,
710: and that D is adjacent to N on the other. Since A, B, D, E are in general position,
711: we can project them to the corners of a square, fig. 8(a). Since C is adjacent to
712: the point of intersection N, that lies at infinity, C is mapped to a point to the right
713: of B as illustrated. Since D is adjacent to N, either F lies to the right of E as in
714: fig. 8(a), or between D and E, as in fig. 8(b). In the first case, X and Z are distinct
715: because neither can be E and X lies on AE and Z lies on CE. The line XZ is either
716: parallel to DE, or not. If not, then it either intersects DE to the right of E or to the
717: left. If it intersects to the right, we can relabel the projective points by swapping
718: A and C, and D and F, and hence X and Z. So, in the first case, without loss of
726:

728: 
729:           (a) A counterexample (E)                (b) A 2nd counterexample (E)
730: 
731: 
732: 
733: 
734:       (c) Perturbing lines AF,CD,XY (E)        (d) Perturbing lines AF,CD,XY (E)
735: 
736:

738: 
739:        (e) Perturbing lines AC,DF (P,E)          (f) Perturbing lines AC,DF (P,E)
762: triangles. To draw fig. 8(f) from 8(d), we add new lines below AC and above DE.
763:    We map the resulting line arrangement back into the projective plane.
764:    So, to summarize, given an arrangement of nine lines in the projective plane, a
765: counterexample to Pappus’s theorem, we can project it onto either fig. 8(a) or 8(b),
766: considered as recording the relative incidence properties of the lines. By a sequence
767: of operations, we can construct a different arrangement of nine lines, in the pro-
768: jective plane, either fig. 8(e) or 8(f), again recording the arrangement of the lines.
769: However, the numbering of the lines in these last two figures, corresponds to the
770: numbering in fig. 6(b), with the same incidence properties, e.g. line 0 crosses lines
771: 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 in order; and line 8 crosses lines 1, 9, 2, 0, 7, 6, 5 and 3 in order;
772: these statements are true of all three diagrams. Hence they represent the same
773: pseudoline arrangement. Thus we have constructed a stretched version of Rin(9),
774: which is not possible: so Pappus is proved.                                                

2232: 16.2. Pseudoline Stretching. Given conjecture 16.16, a combinatoric analysis of
2233: a rank 3 oriented matroid, could start by chosing an arbitrary pseudoline as the
2234: line at infinity, and then find all such twisted graphs, in the resulting projection.
2235: These could be analysed as in this paper, to derive systems of equalities and in-
2236: equalities between sums of products of sines. These could then be analysed using
2237: the techniques of section 10.
2238:    In this way, it is hoped that, an algorithm can be developed that either:
2239:       • Finds a contradiction between the constraints placed on the angles, hencing
2240:          proving nonrealizability
2241:       • Or finds specific angles satisfying all the constraints, hence proving realiz-
2242:          ability (since the resulting linear program in r is necessarily soluble).
2243:    We further conjecture that:
2244: Conjecture 16.17. A rank 3 oriented matroid over n has a biquadratic final poly-
2245: nomial if and only if, there is some choice of a line at infinity, such that the re-
2246: maining n − 1 pseudolines form an arrangment A where there is a subarrangement
2247: B, which is minimally angle constraining and for which Θ(B; A) is empty, i.e. only
2248: one item in the intersection in conjecture 16.15 need be considered.
2249:    We have seen in this paper that this is the case for Rin(9). For the cases for
2250: which no biquadratic final polynomial exists, then more than one item would be
2251: relevant.

2255: suggests a study of Ω+        −

2257: isotopy question, and the question of non-realizable oriented matroids with no bi-
2258: quadratic final polynomial.

2275:                               17. Final Conclusion
2276:    Twisted graphs explain all the examples of counter-examples to Ringel’s slope
2277: conjecture, given at the start of this paper. Figs 2(a) and 2(c) are both twistings
2278: of K4 ; circular saws [Carroll, 2000d] such as fig. 2(b) are twistings of wheel graphs
2279: Wn ; fig. 1(a) has been discussed in depth. Ceva’s theorem, fig. 2(d), is a limiting
2280: case, explicable from the uniform variant, fig. 2(a). This points to the possibility
2281: of a modified statement of Ringel’s conjecture, which gives a complete account of
2282: the slope constraints in a line arrangement, in terms of twisted graphs.
2283:    The key step in this paper was at the beginning, in section 6, with the choice
2284: to use polar coordinates for lines. With this choice, it is clear that for realizable
2285: rank 3 oriented matroids, and an appropriate choice of angles, that a realization
2286: can be found by linear programming, (over the reals, which is harder than over the
2287: rationals).
2288:    Assuming some of the conjectures stated, it may be possible to also use the same
2289: technique for automatically finding non-realizability proofs, for all non-realizable
2290: cases. A specific example of this, the non-Pappus oriented matroid, has been ex-
2291: plored in depth. If this does indeed generalise, then this would also furnish appro-
2292: priate angles in the realizable cases. For oriented matroids where the techniques of
2293: this paper are applicable, the specific proof for that oriented matroid is short, like
2294: the second proof of the main theorem, found on page 41.
2295:    . . . and Pappus was right.
2296:    o rank 3 realizability, [Mnëv, 1988]. I hope that, with an appropriate approach,
2297: this can be seen clearly from within oriented matroid theory, rather than stepping
2298: out to ETR. In particular, the approach taken to proving the non-realizability of

2331:                                   18. Final Conclusion
2332:    Twisted graphs explain all the examples of counter-examples to Ringel’s slope
2333: conjecture, given at the start of this paper. Figs 2(a) and 2(c) are both twistings
2334: of K4 ; circular saws [Carroll, 2000d] such as fig. 2(b) are twistings of wheel graphs
2335: Wn ; fig. 1(a) has been discussed in depth. Ceva’s theorem, fig. 2(d), is a limiting
2336: case, explicable from the uniform variant, fig. 2(a). This points to the possibility
2337: of a modified statement of Ringel’s conjecture, which gives a complete account of
2338: the slope constraints in a line arrangement, in terms of twisted graphs.
2339:    The key step in this paper was at the beginning, in section 6, with the choice
2340: to use polar coordinates for lines. With this choice, it is clear that for realizable
2341: rank 3 oriented matroids, and an appropriate choice of angles, that a realization
2342: can be found by linear programming, (over the reals, which is harder than over the
2343: rationals).
2344:    Assuming some of the conjectures stated, it may be possible to also use the same
2345: technique for automatically finding non-realizability proofs, for all non-realizable
2346: cases. A specific example of this, the non-Pappus oriented matroid, has been ex-
2347: plored in depth. If this does indeed generalise, then this would also furnish appro-
2348: priate angles in the realizable cases. For oriented matroids where the techniques of
2349: this paper are applicable, the specific proof for that oriented matroid is short, like
2350: the second proof of the main theorem, found on page 41.
2351:    . . . and Pappus was right.

18:                                                    The evaluation of the determinants is via a normal form for sums of products
19:                                                    of sines, giving a powerful system of trigonometric identities. The particular
20:                                                    result is generalized to arrangements derived from three edge connected totally
21:                                                    cyclic directed graphs, conjectured to be sufficient for a complete analysis of
22:                                                    angle constraining arrangements of lines, and thus a full response to Ringel’s
23:                                                    slope conjecture. These methods are generally applicable to the realizability
24:                                                    problem for rank 3 oriented matroids.

29:                                                                                 1. Introduction
30:                                               A more accurate, but less snappy, title for this paper might have been: “New
31:                                           Approaches to Angles and Arrangements of Lines and Pseudolines applied to Pap-
32:                                           pus’s Theorem”. This paper does contain a new proof of Pappus’s theorem, but
33:                                           it is fairly laborious and ugly. In particular, it is strangely asymmetric given the
161: these introduces the notion of a twisted graph, generalizing the line arrangement
162: studied in the main theorem. Section 15 shows that results similar to the main
163: theorem can be derived for many twisted graphs, including all totally cyclic, simply
164: three edge connected, directed cubic graphs. Conjectures of stronger results are
165: made.
166:    The final discussion, in section 16, concerns how these results may be relevant to
167: the pseudoline stretching problem, giving (without proof), a cryptomorphic axiom
168: system for rank 3 acyclic oriented matroids, suited for studying partial line arrange-
169: ments in the Euclidean plane. We suggest that every unrealizable rank 3 oriented
170: matroid contains a line arrangement that is a counterexample to Ringel’s slope con-
171: jecture, thus showing that the proof technique used to prove the unstretchability of
172: the non-Pappus arrangement is general. We discuss future directions for this work.
173:    The paper closes with a brief conclusion.
174: 
175:                         3. Context and Related Work
176: 3.1. Euclidean Geometry. Euclidean geometry is the oldest area of mathemat-
177: ical study, but is nowadays seen as essentially completed and not an area for
178: research. One of the claims of this paper is that angles have not been studied
179: adequately independently of the metric in Euclidean geometry (unlike the metric
180: independently of angles). As well as the angular variant of Ceva’s theorem, there
236: 
237: The main result of this paper is equivalent. The invaluable standard reference for
238: oriented matroids is [Björner et al., 1999].
239: 3.4. Ringel’s Slope Conjecture. [Ringel, 1956] conjectured that in an arrange-
240: ment of lines in general position the slopes could be arbitrarily prescribed. This
241: conjecture was disproved first by [Las Vergnas, 1986] using oriented matroid tech-
242: niques over a 32 point dual construction. [Richter-Gebert and Sturmfels, 1991] im-
243: proved this to give a 6 line counterexample (fig. 2(c)), still demonstrating the slope
244: constraint using oriented matroid techniques. [Felsner and Ziegler, 2001] give a
245: different proof of the counterexample using higher Bruhat orders. [Carroll, 2006]
246: demonstrates the result using schoolbook geometry. The main theorem of this pa-
247: per is another counter-example. The theme of the more general analysis of this
248: paper, sections 13 to 16, is the search for all minimal counter-examples to this
249: conjecture.
250: 3.5. Venn Triangles. This paper builds on my earlier work1 which suffers from
251: complete ignorance of the field. This was motivated by a specific pseudoline stretch-
252: ing problem, relating to drawing diagrams of 6-Venn triangles [Carroll, 2000c].
253: While I produced a pseudoline stretching program that stretched the diagrams
254: of interest to me at that time, I could not adequately explain why it worked. This
255: paper is a move towards an explanation. The key insight of my earlier work, that
256: is not in the literature, is that the use of polar coordinates allow the pseudoline
257: stretching problem to be divided into two separate phases: first determine the θ co-
258: ordinates of the lines, and second, use linear programming techniques to determine
259: the r coordinates. The latter step is a solved problem, although issues are presented
705: 8.2. Relationship with Pappus.
706: Proof of Pappus’s theorem 8.3. Let N be the point of intersection of the two lines.
707:    If N, A, B, C, D, E and F are not all distinct, then the theorem is trivial.
708:    Take a counterexample to the theorem.
709:    By relabelling we can assume that A and C are both adjacent to N on one line,
710: and that D is adjacent to N on the other. Since A, B, D, E are in general position,
711: we can project them to the corners of a square, fig. 8(a). Since C is adjacent to
712: the point of intersection N, that lies at infinity, C is mapped to a point to the right
713: of B as illustrated. Since D is adjacent to N, either F lies to the right of E as in
714: fig. 8(a), or between D and E, as in fig. 8(b). In the first case, X and Z are distinct
715: because neither can be E and X lies on AE and Z lies on CE. The line XZ is either
716: parallel to DE, or not. If not, then it either intersects DE to the right of E or to the
717: left. If it intersects to the right, we can relabel the projective points by swapping
718: A and C, and D and F, and hence X and Z. So, in the first case, without loss of
726: 
727: 
728: 
729:           (a) A counterexample (E)                (b) A 2nd counterexample (E)
730: 
731: 
732: 
733: 
734:       (c) Perturbing lines AF,CD,XY (E)        (d) Perturbing lines AF,CD,XY (E)
735: 
736: 
737: 
738: 
739:        (e) Perturbing lines AC,DF (P,E)          (f) Perturbing lines AC,DF (P,E)
762: triangles. To draw fig. 8(f) from 8(d), we add new lines below AC and above DE.
763:    We map the resulting line arrangement back into the projective plane.
764:    So, to summarize, given an arrangement of nine lines in the projective plane, a
765: counterexample to Pappus’s theorem, we can project it onto either fig. 8(a) or 8(b),
766: considered as recording the relative incidence properties of the lines. By a sequence
767: of operations, we can construct a different arrangement of nine lines, in the pro-
768: jective plane, either fig. 8(e) or 8(f), again recording the arrangement of the lines.
769: However, the numbering of the lines in these last two figures, corresponds to the
770: numbering in fig. 6(b), with the same incidence properties, e.g. line 0 crosses lines
771: 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 in order; and line 8 crosses lines 1, 9, 2, 0, 7, 6, 5 and 3 in order;
772: these statements are true of all three diagrams. Hence they represent the same
773: pseudoline arrangement. Thus we have constructed a stretched version of Rin(9),
774: which is not possible: so Pappus is proved.                                                

1667: with a polar frame of reference, if for every a = ({e, f }, {g}) ∈ A, the triangle

1669: a negatively oriented triangle.

2227: Conjecture 16.16. An arrangement is minimally angle constraining if, and only
2228: if, it corresponds to a <-minimal twisted graph, for some <.
2229: 16.2. Pseudoline Stretching. Given conjecture 16.16, a combinatoric analysis of
2230: a rank 3 oriented matroid, could start by chosing an arbitrary pseudoline as the
2231: line at infinity, and then find all such twisted graphs, in the resulting projection.
2232: These could be analysed as in this paper, to derive systems of equalities and in-
2233: equalities between sums of products of sines. These could then be analysed using
2234: the techniques of section 10.
2235:    In this way, it is hoped that, an algorithm can be developed that either:
2236:       • Finds a contradiction between the constraints placed on the angles, hencing
2237:          proving nonrealizability
2238:       • Or finds specific angles satisfying all the constraints, hence proving realiz-
2239:          ability (since the resulting linear program in r is necessarily soluble).
2240:    We further conjecture that:
2241: Conjecture 16.17. A rank 3 oriented matroid over n has a biquadratic final poly-
2242: nomial if and only if, there is some choice of a line at infinity, such that the re-
2243: maining n − 1 pseudolines form an arrangment A where there is a subarrangement
2244: B, which is minimally angle constraining and for which Θ(B; A) is empty, i.e. only
2245: one item in the intersection in conjecture 16.15 need be considered.
2246:    We have seen in this paper that this is the case for Rin(9). For the cases for
2247: which no biquadratic final polynomial exists, then more than one item would be
2248: relevant.

2250: 16.3. Isotopy Problems. The above discussion, also raises the hope that these
2251: techniques may help explain oriented matroids with non-isotopic realizations. This
2252: suggests a study of Ω+        −

2254: isotopy question, and the question of non-realizable oriented matroids with no bi-
2255: quadratic final polynomial.
2271: 
2272:                               17. Final Conclusion
2273:    Twisted graphs explain all the examples of counter-examples to Ringel’s slope
2274: conjecture, given at the start of this paper. Figs 2(a) and 2(c) are both twistings
2275: of K4 ; circular saws [Carroll, 2000d] such as fig. 2(b) are twistings of wheel graphs
2276: Wn ; fig. 1(a) has been discussed in depth. Ceva’s theorem, fig. 2(d), is a limiting
2277: case, explicable from the uniform variant, fig. 2(a). This points to the possibility
2278: of a modified statement of Ringel’s conjecture, which gives a complete account of
2279: the slope constraints in a line arrangement, in terms of twisted graphs.
2280:    The key step in this paper was at the beginning, in section 6, with the choice
2281: to use polar coordinates for lines. With this choice, it is clear that for realizable
2282: rank 3 oriented matroids, and an appropriate choice of angles, that a realization
2283: can be found by linear programming, (over the reals, which is harder than over the
2284: rationals).
2285:    Assuming some of the conjectures stated, it may be possible to also use the same
2286: technique for automatically finding non-realizability proofs, for all non-realizable
2287: cases. A specific example of this, the non-Pappus oriented matroid, has been ex-
2288: plored in depth. If this does indeed generalise, then this would also furnish appro-
2289: priate angles in the realizable cases. For oriented matroids where the techniques of
2290: this paper are applicable, the specific proof for that oriented matroid is short, like
2291: the second proof of the main theorem, found on page 41.
2292:    . . . and Pappus was right.
2293:    o rank 3 realizability, [Mnëv, 1988]. I hope that, with an appropriate approach,
2294: this can be seen clearly from within oriented matroid theory, rather than stepping
2295: out to ETR. In particular, the approach taken to proving the non-realizability of
2313: at school.

2329:    Twisted graphs explain all the examples of counter-examples to Ringel’s slope
2330: conjecture, given at the start of this paper. Figs 2(a) and 2(c) are both twistings
2331: of K4 ; circular saws [Carroll, 2000d] such as fig. 2(b) are twistings of wheel graphs
2332: Wn ; fig. 1(a) has been discussed in depth. Ceva’s theorem, fig. 2(d), is a limiting
2333: case, explicable from the uniform variant, fig. 2(a). This points to the possibility
2334: of a modified statement of Ringel’s conjecture, which gives a complete account of
2335: the slope constraints in a line arrangement, in terms of twisted graphs.
2336:    The key step in this paper was at the beginning, in section 6, with the choice
2337: to use polar coordinates for lines. With this choice, it is clear that for realizable
2338: rank 3 oriented matroids, and an appropriate choice of angles, that a realization
2339: can be found by linear programming, (over the reals, which is harder than over the
2340: rationals).
2341:    Assuming some of the conjectures stated, it may be possible to also use the same
2342: technique for automatically finding non-realizability proofs, for all non-realizable
2343: cases. A specific example of this, the non-Pappus oriented matroid, has been ex-
2344: plored in depth. If this does indeed generalise, then this would also furnish appro-
2345: priate angles in the realizable cases. For oriented matroids where the techniques of
2346: this paper are applicable, the specific proof for that oriented matroid is short, like
2347: the second proof of the main theorem, found on page 41.
2348:    . . . and Pappus was right.

67:                Is it possible for the number of length k patterns of each type to
68: be exactly nk /k! ? The case k = 1 is trivial, while k = 2 again requires a bit of
69: thought, and k = 3 is far from obvious. We show that there do exist permutations
70: satisfying this condition when k = 3. Again, k = 4 remains open. And, again, this
71: problem very closely parallels one in graph theory: can all the k-vertex subgraph
72: counts be exactly equal? Janson and Spencer consider this problem in [10] and,
73: intriguingly, are also able to resolve the question (in the positive) only for k = 1,
74: 2, and 3.
75:    Certainly, a necessary condition is that the quantity nk /k! be an integer. Hence,

385:                   4. Asymptotic Symmetry and Inflatability
386:    In this section, we investigate the possibility of using a “blow-up” construction
387: to address Graham’s conjecture. First, define a sequence of permutations σi ∈ Sni ,
388: ni → ∞, to be “asymptotically k-symmetric” if, for each τ ∈ Sk ,
389:                                     k!2 Xτ (σi )
390:                                      lim         = 1.
391:                               i→∞       nk
392: Customarily droppingindices, we may state asymptotic k-perfection as the condi-
393: tion that Xτ (σ) = nk (1/k! + o(1)) for each τ ∈ Sk . We write sym′k (σ) to mean
394: that the sequence σi is asymptotically k-symmetric.
395: Question 2 (Graham). Does there exist a k ≥ 1 so that
396:                                    sym′k (σ) ⇒ sym′k+1 (σ)?
397:     We define a tensor product ⊗ of permutations, corresponding to the Kronecker
409:     Let ρn be a uniformly distributed random permutation on n symbols. We believe
410: it very likely that permutations σ exist so that σ ⊗ρn is asymptotically k-symmetric
411: but not asymptotically (k+1)-symmetric. Evidently, the existence of such permuta-
412: tions would resolve Graham’s conjecture in the negative. We begin our description
413: of these σ with k = 2.
414: Proposition 8. For σ ∈ Sm , m fixed, if the permutation σ ⊗ ρn is asymptotically
415: 2-symmetric, then σ is 2-symmetric.
416: Proof. Let πn = σ ⊗ ρn . Suppose that sym′2 (πn ), i.e.,
417:                      X(12) (πn ) = X(21) (πn ) = (1 + o(1))(mn)2 /4.

609: Theorem 2.27. Let A be an X × Y P-matrix. Then A is a scaled P[Cr(A)]-
610: matrix.
611: 
612: Proof. Let A be a counterexample with |X |+|Y | minimal, and define P′ :=
613: P[Cr(A)]. Without loss of generality we assume that A is normalized with
614: normalizing spanning forest T .
615: Claim 2.27.1. If every entry of A is in P′ and A′ ∼ A is T ′ -normalized for
616: some maximal spanning forest T ′ then every entry of A′ is in P′ .
617:   Proof. We prove this for the case T ′ = (T \ x y)∪ x ′ y ′ for edges x y, x ′ y ′
618:   with x, x ′ ∈ X and y, y ′ ∈ Y . The claim follows by induction. Without
619:   loss of generality assume T, T ′ are trees. Let X 1 ∪ Y1 , X 2 ∪ Y2 be the
620:   components of T \ e such that x ∈ X 1 , y ∈ Y2 . Let p := A x ′ y ′ . Then A′
621:   is the matrix obtained from A by multiplying all entries in A[X , Y2 ] by
622:   p−1 and all entries in A[X 2 , Y ] by p. Since p ∈ P′ the claim follows.

2007: 6     A number of questions and conjectures
2008: The following conjecture links fundamental elements and universal par-
2009: tial fields.
2010: Conjecture 6.1. If PN has finitely many fundamental elements, then all
2011: PN -representations of N are equivalent.
2012:     This conjecture cannot be strengthened by much. Consider the ma-
2013: troid M [I A3 ] from Table 1, which is obtained from the Fano matroid by
2014:                                                                (2)    (2)
2015: adding one element freely to a line. The homomorphism ϕ : U1 → U1
2016: determined by x 7→ x 2 is not an automorphism. A related conjecture is
2017: the following:
2018: Conjecture 6.2. If N is 3-connected then N is a PN -stabilizer for the class
2019: of PN -representable matroids.
2020:     Even if this is only true when N is uniquely PN -representable this con-
2021: jecture would have important implications. For example a theorem by
2022: Geelen, Gerards, and Whittle [9] would follow immediately and could,
2023: in fact, be strengthened.
2024:     Not all partial fields are universal. For instance, it is not hard to con-
2025: struct partial fields with homomorphisms to GF(3) different from the ones
2026: in Theorem 5.1.
2027: Question 6.3. What distinguishes universal partial fields from partial fields
2028: in general?
2044: matroids. A partial field P is M -universal if, for every partial field P′
2045: such that every matroid in M is P′ -representable, there exists a homo-
2046: morphism ϕ : P → P′ .
2047: Conjecture 6.6. Let M be the set of all P-representable matroids, where P
2048: is a level partial field. Then P is M -universal.
2049:    As mentioned before, the Settlement Theorem is reminiscent of the
2050: theory of free expansions from Geelen et al. [12]. We offer the following
2051: conjecture:
2052: Conjecture 6.7. Let M be a representable matroid. Then M \e settles M if
2053: and only if e is fixed in M .

21:                                                                       1. Introduction
22:                                              Lusztig’s conjecture on the irreducible rational characters of a reduc-
23:                                           tive algebraic group over a field k of positive characteristic has in the
24:                                           last 20 years been approached from various directions. It is known that
25:                                           Lusztig’s formula holds for large enough characteristics (with respect
26:                                           to a fixed root system), yet so far it is unknown what “large enough”
27:                                           means in all but low rank cases (conjecturally, the characteristic should
28:                                           be at least the Coxeter number of the root system). So in almost all ex-
29:                                           plicitely given cases the irreducible characters of a reductive group over
30:                                           k are unknown. This is certainly not a completely satisfying situation.
31: 
32:                                           1.1. Lusztig’s conjecture as a moment graph problem. The most
33:                                           recent approach towards Lusztig’s conjecture, contained in the articles
34:                                           [Fie4] and [Fie5], gives a connection between representations of reduc-
35:                                           tive groups over k (more precisely, representations of their Lie alge-
36:                                           bras) and the theory of k-sheaves on moment graphs. In particular,
37:                                           it is shown in the above papers that Lusztig’s conjecture follows from
38:                                           a similar multiplicity conjecture for the Braden-MacPherson sheaves
39:                                           (with coefficients in the field k) on the associated affine moment graph.
40:                                              The moment graph theory has the advantage that it can be formu-
41:                                           lated and studied in a relatively elementary way. In particular, one can
42:                                           determine the p-smooth locus on an affine moment graph by quite el-
43:                                           ementary arguments (cf. [Fie3]). The result is in complete accordance
44:                                           with Lusztig’s conjecture and yields its multiplicity one case for all
45:                                                                                  1
46: 
47: 2       AN UPPER BOUND ON EXCEPTIONAL CHARACTERISTICS
48: 
49: fields with characteristic at least the Coxeter number. Unfortunately,
50: this cannot be reinterpreted in terms of characters.
51: 1.2. A Lefschetz theory for sheaves on moment graphs. In the
52: present paper we study a Lefschetz theory for the moment graph and
53: show that the multiplicity conjecture is implied by a Hard Lefschetz
54: conjecture for the Braden-MacPherson sheaves. Then we prove the
55: latter conjecture in the characteristic zero case following the argumen-
56: tation in [Soe2], where the equivalent category of special bimodules is
57: considered. The essential ingredient for the proof is the fact that these
58: bimodules occur as equivariant intersection cohomologies of Schubert
59: varieties. The Hard Lefschetz conjecture for bimodules then follows
60: from the Hard Lefschetz theorem for projective varieties.
61: 1.3. From Q to Fp via Z. In positive characteristic, Soergel’s ap-
62: proach does not work any more for several reasons, one of them being
63: that the correspondence between Braden-MacPherson sheaves and in-
64: tersection cohomology cannot be established so far. Yet one can deduce
65: the conjecture for big enough characteristics from its characteristic zero
66: analogue. For this one first replaces the Braden-MacPherson sheaves
67: by their global sections which naturally are modules over the (com-
68: mutative, associative) structure algebra Z associated to the graph.
69: Then one translates the multiplicity conjecture into the language of
70: Z-modules. The advantage now is that there is an alternative way
71: to construct the global sections as direct summands of Bott–Samelson
72: modules. These modules turn out to be defined over the integers, so
73: that we can apply a base change argument. A closer look at the base
74: change procedure opens a way to give an estimate on the exceptional
75: primes. In the particular case that is connected to Lusztig’s conjecture
76: we get the following:
77: Theorem. Suppose that char k > r!(r!(r−1)!N(wb0 )l(wb0 )+2d )r . Then the
78: Hard Lefschetz conjecture for the Braden-MacPherson sheaves, hence
79: Lusztig’s conjecture, holds.
80:    Here, wb0 is the element in the affine Weyl group corresponding to the
81: lowest alcove in the anti-fundamental box, l is the affine length function,
82: and r, d and N(w  b0 ) are numbers that can be explicitely computed inside
83: the affine Hecke algebra (cf. Section 5.3). Admittedly, the above bound
84: is very far from the Coxeter number. It is, however, an explicit number
85: and in the general case the only bound that is known to the author.
86:    After having established the Lefschetz theory, we derive the above
87: estimate using the most basic linear algebra. I am sure that any careful
88: reader can immediately come up with an improved bound. I strongly
89:

100:             2. Sheaves on the affine Bruhat graph
101:   In this section we introduce the main players, the Braden–MacPherson
102: sheaves on an affine moment graph. We state the main conjecture on
103: the graded rank of their stalks and quickly review the connection to
104: Lusztig’s conjecture on simple rational characters of reductive groups.
105: As a first step, let us recall the notion of a moment graph.
106: 
107: 2.1. Moment graphs. Let Y ∼
108:                           = Zr be a lattice.
109: Definition 2.1. A moment graph G = (V, E, α, ⩽) over Y is given by
110: the following data.
111:    (1) A graph (V, E) with set of vertices V and set of edges E.
112:    (2) A map α : E → Y \ {0}, called the labelling.
113:    (3) A partial order “⩽” on V such that two vertices are comparable
114:         if they are connected by an edge.

397: 2.12. Lusztig’s conjecture. Suppose that k is a field of positive non-
398: even characteristic. Let k be an algebraic closure of k and let G∨k be the

405:    The main result in [Fie4] and [Fie5] is that Conjecture 2.9 implies
406: Lusztig’s conjecture on the characters of irreducible rational represen-
407: tations of G∨k . More precisely:
408: Theorem 2.10. Suppose that char k is at least the Coxeter number of
409: R. If Conjecture 2.9 holds for all w ⩽ w b0 , then Lusztig’s conjecture on
410: the characters of irreducible rational representations of Gk∨ holds.

616:      4. A Lefschetz theory for sheaves on moment graphs
617:    We approach Conjecture 2.9 by studying an analogue of the Hard
618: Lefschetz property of the intersection cohomology of projective varieties.
619: For this we have to replace the symmetric algebra S by a polynomial
620: ring in one variable.
621:    So let us fix w ∈ W  c and a field K. Let K[t] be the polynomial
622: ring in one variable over K. We consider K[t] as a graded algebra with
623: deg t = 2. Let us fix, in addition, a graded homomorphism τ : S → K[t]
624: of K-algebras that has the property that τ (α) 6= 0 for all α ∈ Rb+ that
625: appear as a label of an edge of Gb≤w .
626:    For example, we might take the following map τ . Let us define the
627: height ht(α) of a positive affine root α ∈ Rb+ as the number n such that

703: Conjecture 4.4. Suppose that (K, w) is a GKM-pair and that char K >
704: N(w). Then for all x ≤ w the map
705:                            ix : B(w)x → B(w)x
706: satisfies the Lefschetz condition with center l(w).
707: Theorem 4.5. For each pair (K, w), Conjecture 4.4 implies Conjecture
708: 2.9.
709: Proof. Suppose that Conjecture 4.4 holds for the pair (K, w) and let
710: x < w. Then B(w)x /B(w)x is a direct sum of K[t]-modules of the form
711: K[t]/hta i{−l(w) + a} for various a > 0. By Proposition 4.2 only terms
712: with a > l(x) occur. Now K[t]/hta i{−l(w) + a} is generated in degree
713: l(w) − a, which is strictly smaller than l(w) − l(x), and we deduce
714: that B(w)x is generated in degrees < l(w) − l(x). This statement is
715: equivalent to Conjecture 2.9 by Proposition 3.10.                    

722: Sketch of proof. The proof is essentially contained in the last section

726: sional parabolic situation is considered, while we need to work in the

728: are still finite dimensional, so there are no further technical difficulties
729: to overcome. For the Kac-Moody versions of the well-known results
730: about Schubert varieties see [Kum].

752:   5. From characteristic zero to positive characteristic
753:    The main application of the Lefschetz theory developed in this article
754: is a calculation of an upper bound for the exceptional characteristics
755: for the Lusztig’s conjecture, i.e. we give an explicit bound such that
756: for all K with larger characteristic Conjecture 4.4 holds. Of course,
757: we use a base change argument and in particular we have to develop
758: a version of the Lefschetz theory over the integers. It turns out that
759: the Bott–Samelson modules can indeed be defined over Z (cf. Section
760: 6). Before we introduce this construction, we associate to each Bott–
761: Samelson module its Lefschetz data and prove Proposition 5.3 which
762: serves us as a bridge from characteristic zero to positive characteristics.
763: 5.1. Lefschetz data. Suppose that f : A → B is as in Section 4.1.
764: Definition 5.1. Suppose that (a1 , b1 ), . . . , (an , bn ) ∈ N × Z are such
765: that
766:                             M n
795: x < w and let ((a1 , b1 ), . . . , (an , bn )) be the Lefschetz datum of BK (s) at
796: x. Then the number of i such that xi = x equals the number of j such
797: that aj = l(x).
798: Proposition 5.3. Suppose that the Conjecture 4.4 holds for BK (w ′)
799: for all w ′ < w and that the Lefschetz data of BK (s) and BQ (s) coincide
800: at all x ≤ w. Then Conjecture 4.4 holds for BK (w).
801: Proof. Lemma 5.2 implies that the decomposition of BQ (s) into a di-
802: rect sum of shifted copies of BQ (y) for various y ≤ w parallels the
803: analogous decomposition of BK (s). By our assumption, for all w ′ < w
804: the Lefschetz data of BQ (w ′) at all x < w ′ equals the corresponding
805: Lefschetz data of BK (w ′). Since BK (w) occurs as a direct summand in
806: BK (s) with multiplicity one and else only shifted BK (w ′) with w ′ < w
807: occur, the Lefschetz data of BK (w) and BQ (w) coincide. Hence we de-
808: duce Conjecture 4.4 for BK (w) from the corresponding claim for BQ (w)
809: which we have proven in Theorem 4.6.                                   

22:                                          algebraic group over a field k of positive characteristic has in the last 20 years
23:                                          been approached from various directions. It is known that Lusztig’s formula
24:                                          holds for large enough characteristics (with respect to a fixed root system),
25:                                          yet so far it is unknown what “large enough” means in all but low rank cases
26:                                          (conjecturally, the characteristic should be at least the Coxeter number of the
27:                                          root system). So in almost all explicitely given cases the irreducible characters
28:                                          of a reductive group are unknown. This is certainly not a completely satisfying
29:                                          situation.

33:                                          cent approach towards Lusztig’s conjecture, contained in the articles [Fie4]
34:                                          and [Fie5], gives a connection between representations of reductive groups
35:                                          over k (more precisely, representations of their Lie algebras) and the theory
36:                                          of k-sheaves on moment graphs. In particular, it is shown in the above pa-
37:                                          pers that Lusztig’s conjecture follows from a similar multiplicity conjecture for
38:                                          the Braden-MacPherson sheaves (with coefficients in the field k) on an affine
39:                                          moment graph.
40:                                             The moment graph theory has the advantage that it can be formulated and
41:                                          studied in a relatively elementary way. In particular, one can determine the
42:                                          p-smooth locus on an affine moment graph by quite elementary arguments
43:                                          (cf. [Fie3]). The result is in complete accordance with Lusztig’s conjecture
44:                                          and yields its multiplicity one case for all fields with characteristic at least
45:                                          the Coxeter number. Unfortunately, this cannot be reinterpreted in terms of
46:                                          characters.

50:                                          .

53: 2           AN UPPER BOUND ON EXCEPTIONAL CHARACTERISTICS
57: 
58: 1.2. A Lefschetz theory for sheaves on moment graphs. In the present
59: paper we study a Lefschetz theory for moment graphs and show that the mul-
60: tiplicity conjecture is implied by a Hard Lefschetz conjecture for the Braden-
61: MacPherson sheaves. Then we prove the latter conjecture in the characteristic
62: zero case following the argumentation in [Soe3], where the equivalent category
63: of special bimodules is considered. The essential ingredient for the proof is the
64: fact that our objects occur as equivariant intersection cohomologies of Schu-
65: bert varieties. The Hard Lefschetz conjecture for moment graphs then follows
66: from the Hard Lefschetz theorem for projective varieties.
67: 
68: 1.3. From Q to Fp via Z. In positive characteristic, Soergel’s approach does
69: not work any more for several reasons, one of them being that the corre-
70: spondence between Braden-MacPherson sheaves and intersection cohomology
71: cannot be established so far. Yet one can deduce the conjecture for big enough
72: characteristics from its characteristic zero analogue. For this one first replaces
73: the Braden-MacPherson sheaves by their global sections which naturally are
74: modules over the (commutative, associative) structure algebra Z associated to
75: the graph. Then one translates the multiplicity conjecture into the language
76: of Z-modules. The advantage is that there is an alternative way to construct
77: those global sections as direct summands of Bott–Samelson modules. These
78: turn out to be defined over the integers, so that we can apply a base change
79: argument. A closer look at the base change procedure opens a way to give an
80: estimate on the exceptional primes.
81:    In the particular case that is connected to Lusztig’s conjecture we get the
82: following result. For a fixed root system R we let w   b0 be the element in the
83: affine Weyl group corresponding to the lowest alcove in the anti-fundamental
84: box and we let l = l(w b0 ) be its length. For any reduced expression s of wb0 we
85: define the numbers r = r(s), d = d(s) and N = N(s) explicitly in terms of the
86: affine Hecke algebra (cf. Section 5.3). Then we set
87: 
88:                        b0 ) := min r!(r!(r − 1)!N l(wb0 )+2d )r .
89:                      U(w
90:                                   s
91: 
92: Theorem. Suppose that char k > U(w     b0 ). Then the Hard Lefschetz conjec-
93: ture for the Braden-MacPherson sheaves, hence Lusztig’s conjecture for the
94: algebraic groups with root system R, holds.
95: 
96:    Admittedly, the above bound is very far from the Coxeter number. It is,
97: however, an explicit number and in the general case the only bound that is
98: known to the author.
99:    After having established the Lefschetz theory, we derive the above estimate
100: using the most basic linear algebra. I am sure that any careful reader can
101: immediately come up with an improved bound. I strongly doubt, however,
102: that the methods outlined in this paper suffice to come anywhere in the vicinity
103: of the Coxeter number.

459: the characters of irreducible rational representations of G∨k holds.

924:       5. From characteristic zero to positive characteristic
925:    The main application of the Lefschetz theory developed in this article is a
926: calculation of an upper bound for the exceptional characteristics for Lusztig’s
927: conjecture. For this we use a base change argument. So we have to develop a
928: version of the Lefschetz theory over the integers. It turns out that the Bott–
929: Samelson modules can indeed be defined over Z (cf. Section 6). Before we
930: introduce this construction, we associate to each Bott–Samelson module its
931: Lefschetz data and prove Proposition 5.4 which serves us as a bridge from
932: characteristic zero to positive characteristics.

24:                                                                                   1. Introduction
25:                                             Let us first recall the famous Goldbach conjecture in additive number theory.
26:                                           Conjecture 1.1 (Goldbach’s Conjecture). Any even integer n ⩾ 4 can be written as the
27:                                           sum of two primes.
28:                                              The number of primes not exceeding n ⩾ 2 is approximately n/ log n by the prime
29:                                           number theorem. Hardy and Littlewood conjectured that the number of ways to write an
30:                                           even integer n ⩾ 4 as the sum of two primes is given asymptotically by
31:                                                                                                   
32:                                                                              cn Y               1
33:                                                                                          1+          ,
34:                                                                            log2 n p|n         p−2
35: 
36:                                           where c = 2 p (1 − (p − 1)−2 ) = 1.3203 · · · is a constant and p runs over odd primes. (Cf.
37:                                                       Q
38: 
39:                                           [7, pp. 159-164].)
40:                                              Goldbach’s conjecture remains open, and the best result in this direction is Chen’s
41:                                           theorem (cf. [1]): Each large even integer can be written as the sum of a prime and a
42:                                           product of at most two primes.
43:                                              Those integers Tx√= x(x+1)/2 with x ∈ N = {0, 1, 2, . . .} are called triangular numbers.
44:                                           There are less than 2n positive triangular numbers below an integer n ⩾ 2, so triangular
45:                                           numbers are more sparse than prime numbers. In 2008 the author made the following
46:                                           conjecture.
47:                                           Conjecture 1.2 (Sun [22]). (i) Each natural number n 6= 216 can be written in the form
48:                                           p + Tx with x ∈ N, where p is zero or a prime.
49:                                             (ii) Any odd integer greater than 3 can be written in the form p + x(x + 1), where p is
50:                                           a prime and x is positive integer.
51: 
52:                                             Key words and phrases. Mixed sum, prime, Fibonacci number, linear recurrence, representation.
53:                                             2000 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 11P32; Secondary 11A41, 11B37, 11B39, 11B75,
54:                                           11Y99.
55:                                             Research supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (grant 10871087).
56:                                                                                              1
57: 
61: 10 and 1012 respectively. The author [23] would like to offer 1000 US dollars for the first
62:     10
63: 
64: positive solutions to both (i) and (ii), and $200 for the first explicit counterexample to (i)
65: or (ii).
66:    Powers of two are even much more sparse than triangular numbers. In a letter to
67: Goldbach, Euler posed the problem whether any odd integer n > 1 can be expressed in
68: the form p+2a , where p is a prime and a ∈ N. This question was reformulated by Polignac
69: in 1849. By introducing covers of the integers by residue classes, Erdős [4] showed that
70: there exists an infinite arithmetic progression of positive odd integers no term of which is
71: of the form p + 2a . (See also Nathanson [14, pp. 204-208].) On the basis of the work of
72: Cohen and Selfridge [2], the author [17] proved that if
73:                        x ≡ 47867742232066880047611079 (mod M)
74: with
142: [25] were able to construct a residue class containing no integers of the form pa + F3n /2
143: with p a prime and a, n ∈ N. Note that un = F3n /2 is just half of an even Fibonacci
144: number; also u0 = 0, u1 = 1, and un+1 = 4un + un−1 for n = 1, 2, 3, . . ..
145:   On December 23, 2008 the author [19] formulated the following conjecture.
146: Conjecture 1.5 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Fibonacci Numbers). Any integer
147: n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime and two positive Fibonacci numbers. We
148: can require further that one of the two Fibonacci numbers is odd.
149: Remark 1.6. For a large integer n, there are about log n/ log ϕ Fibonacci numbers below
150: n but there are about n/ log n primes below n. So, Fibonacci numbers are much more
151: sparse than prime numbers and hence the above conjecture looks more difficult than the
152: Goldbach conjecture. D. McNeil (cf. [12, 13]) has verified Conjecture 1.5 up to 1014 . The
153: author (cf. [23]) would like to offer 5000 US dollars for the first positive solution published
154: in a well-known mathematical journal and $250 for the first explicit counterexample which
155: can be rechecked by the author via computer. Note that Conjecture 1.5 implies that for
156: any odd prime p we can find an odd prime q < p such that p − q can be written as the
157: sum of two odd Fibonacci numbers.
158:

161:     Recall that the Pell sequence {Pn }n⩾0 is defined as follows.
162:                 P0 = 0, P1 = 1, and Pn+1 = 2Pn + Pn−1 for n = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
163: It is well known that
164:                          1            √ n     √ n

172:    On Jan. 10, 2009, the author [20] posed the following conjecture which is an analogue
173: of Conjecture 1.5.
174: Conjecture 1.7 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Pell Numbers). Any integer n > 5
175: can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a Pell number and twice a Pell number. We
176: can require further that the two Pell numbers are positive.
177: Remark 1.8. D. McNeil (cf. [23]) has verified Conjecture 1.7 up to 5 ∗ 1013 and found
178: no counterexample. The author (cf. [23]) would like to offer 1000 US dollars for the first
179: positive solution published in a well-known mathematical journal and $100 for the first
180: explicit counterexample which can be rechecked by the author via computer.
181:   Soon after he learned Conjecture 1.7 from the author, Qing-Hu Hou (Nankai University)
182: observed (without proof) that all the sums Ps +2Pt (s, t = 1, 2, 3, . . .) are distinct. Clearly
183: Hou’s observation follows from our following theorem.
184: Theorem 1.9. Let a > 1 be an integer, and set
185:                   u0 = 0, u1 = 1, and ui+1 = aui + ui−1 for i = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
186: Then no integer x can be written as um + aun (with m ∈ N and n ∈ Z+ ) in at least two
187: ways, except in the case a = 2 and x = u0 + au2 = u2 + au1 = 4.
188: Remark 1.10. Note that if n ∈ Z+ then un+1 + au0 = aun + un−1.
189: Corollary 1.11. Let k, l, m, n ∈ Z+ . Then Pk + 2Pl = Pm + 2Pn if and only if k = m
190: and l = n.
191: Remark 1.12. In view of Corollary 1.11, we can assign an ordered pair hm, ni ∈ Z+ ×Z+
193: called a Sidon sequence if all the sums of pairs, ai + aj , are all distinct. An unsolved
194: problem of Erdős (cf. [7, p. 403]) asks for a polynomial P (x) ∈ Z[x] such that all the
195: sums P (m) + P (n) (0 ⩽ m < n) are distinct.
196:    Motivated by Conjecture 1.5 and its variants, Qing-Hu Hou and Jiang Zeng (University
197: of Lyon-I) formulated the following conjecture during their visit to the author in Jan. 2009.
198: Conjecture 1.13 (Hou and Zeng [9]). Any integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of
199: an odd prime, a positive Fibonacci number and a Catalan number.
200: 
201:                        MIXED SUMS OF PRIMES AND OTHER TERMS                              5

203: Remark 1.14. Catalan numbers are integers of the form
204:                                                
205:                        1    2n       2n         2n
206:                 Cn =             =        −           (n ∈ N),
207:                       n+1 n           n        n+1
208: which play important roles in combinatorics (see, e.g., Stanley [16, Chapter 6]). They are
212:                                   k=0
213:                                   √
214: By Stirling’s formula, Cn ∼ 4n /(n nπ) as n → +∞. D. McNeil [13] has verified Conjec-
215: ture 1.13 up to 3 × 1013 and found no counterexample. Hou and Zeng would like to offer
216: 1000 US dollars for the first positive solution published in a well-known mathematical
217: journal and $200 for the first explicit counterexample which can be rechecked by them
218: via computer. Note that 3627586 cannot be written in the form p + 2Fs + Ct with p a
219: prime and s, t ∈ N.
220:   The Lucas sequence {Ln }n⩾0 is defined as follows.
221:                 L0 = 2, L1 = 1, and Ln+1 = Ln + Ln−1 (n = 1, 2, 3, . . .).

228:    On Jan. 16, 2009 the author (cf. [20]) made the following conjecture which is similar
229: to Conjecture 1.13.
230: Conjecture 1.15. Each integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a
231: Lucas number and a Catalan number.
232: Remark 1.16. D. McNeil [13] has verified Conjecture 1.15 up to 1013 and found no
233: counterexample. Note that 1389082 cannot be written in the form p + 2Ls + Ct with p a
234: prime and s, t ∈ N.

368:                  3. Discussion of Conjecture 1.5 and its variants
369:    Concerning Conjecture 1.5, we mention that there are very few natural numbers not
370: representable as the sum of a prime p ≡ 5 (mod 6) and two Fibonacci numbers. Bjorn
371: Poonen (MIT) informed me that by a heuristic argument there should be infinitely many
372: positive integers not in the form p + Fs + Ft if we require that the prime p lies in a fixed
373: residue classe with modulus greater than one. McNeil [11, 13] made a computer search
374: to find natural numbers not representable as the sum of a prime p ≡ 5 (mod 6) and
375: two Fibonacci numbers; he found that there are totally 734 such numbers in the interval
376: [0, 1014 ], the largest of which is 99960670052476, and the smallest one greater than 4 is
377: 857530546.
378:    In 2008 the author (cf. [19, 20]) also made the following conjecture which is similar to
379: Conjecture 1.5.
380: Conjecture 3.1. (i) Any integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a
381: positive Fibonacci number and the square of a positive Fibonacci number. We can require
382: further that one of the two Fibonacci numbers is odd.
383:   (ii) Each integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a positive Fibonacci
384: number and the cube of a positive Fibonacci number. We can require further that one of
385: the two Fibonacci numbers is odd.
386: Remark 3.2. Note that 900068 cannot be written as the sum of a prime, a Fibonacci
387: number and the fourth power of a Fibonacci number. Also,

405:             s1 (1050 + 39030) = 2.22359 · · · and s1 (1050 + 5864) = 2.29037 · · · .
406:     Here is another variant of Conjecture 1.5 made by the author (cf. [19, 21]).
407: Conjecture 3.3. (i) Any integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, an
408: odd Lucas number and a positive Lucas number. For k = 2, 3 we can write any integer
409: n > 4 in the form p + Ls + Lkt , where p is an odd prime, s, t ⩾ 0, and Ls or Lt is odd.
410: 
411:                         MIXED SUMS OF PRIMES AND OTHER TERMS                                   9
412: 
413:   (ii) Each integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a positive Fibonacci
414: number and twice a positive Fibonacci number (or half of a positive Fibonacci number).
415: We can also represent any integer n > 4 as the sum of an odd prime, twice a positive
416: Fibonacci number, and the square of a positive Fibonacci number.
417:   (iii) Any integer n > 4 can be written in the form p + Fs + Lt with p an odd prime,
418: s > 0, and Fs or Lt odd.
419: Remark 3.4. The author verified Conjectures 3.1 and 3.3 for n ⩽ 3 × 107 . Qing-Hu Hou
420: found that 17540144 cannot be written as the sum of a prime, a Lucas number and the
421: fourth power of a Lucas number. McNeil (cf. [12]) has verified the first assertions in parts
422: (i) and (ii) of Conjectures 3.1 and 3.3 up to 1012 . He (cf. [13]) has also verified part (iii)
423: of Conjecture 3.3 up to 1013 , and found that 36930553345551 cannot be written as the
424: sum of a prime, a Fibonacci number and an even Lucas number.
425:   What about the representations n = p+Ps +kPt with k ∈ {1, 3, 4} related to Conjecture
426: 1.7? Note that 2176 cannot be written as the sum of a prime and two Pell numbers. McNeil
427: [13] found that 393185153350 cannot be written as the sum of a prime, a Pell number
428: and three times a Pell number, and the smallest integer greater than 7 not representable
429: as the sum of a prime, a Pell number and four times a Pell number is
430:                                  872377759846 ≈ 8.7 × 1011 .
431: The companion Pell sequence {Qn }n⩾0 is defined by
432:                   Q0 = Q1 = 2 and Qn+1 = 2Qn + Qn−1 (n = 1, 2, 3, . . .).
433: McNeil [13] found that the smallest integer greater than 5 not representable as the sum
434: of a prime, a Pell number and a companion Pell number is 169421772576.
435:    McNeil’s counterexamples seem to suggest that Conjecture 1.7 might also have large
436: counterexamples. However, in the author’s opinion, the large counterexamples to the
437: representations n = p + Ps + 3Pt and n = p + Ps + 4Pt hint that they are very close to the
438: “truth” (Conjecture 1.7). Corollary 1.11 is also a good evidence to support Conjecture
439: 1.7. To expel suspicion, the author has investigated the behavior of the representation
440: function

22:                                                                                    1. Introduction
23:                                             Let us first recall the famous Goldbach conjecture in additive number theory.
24:                                           Conjecture 1.1 (Goldbach’s Conjecture). Any even integer n ⩾ 4 can be written as the
25:                                           sum of two primes.
26:                                             The number of primes not exceeding n ⩾ 2 is approximately n/ log n by the prime
27:                                           number theorem. Hardy and Littlewood conjectured that the number of ways to write an
28:                                           even integer n ⩾ 4 as the sum of two primes is given asymptotically by
29:                                                                                               
30:                                                                             cn Y            1
31:                                                                                      1+          ,
32:                                                                           log2 n          p−2
33:                                                                                         p|n
34: 
35:                                           where c = 2 p (1 − (p − 1)−2 ) = 1.3203 · · · is a constant and p runs over odd primes. (Cf.
36:                                                        Q
37: 
38:                                           [7, pp. 159-164].)
39:                                              Goldbach’s conjecture remains open, and the best result in this direction is Chen’s
40:                                           theorem (cf. [1]): Each large even integer can be written as the sum of a prime and a
41:                                           product of at most two primes.
42:                                              Those integers Tx√= x(x+1)/2 with x ∈ N = {0, 1, 2, . . .} are called triangular numbers.
43:                                           There are less than 2n positive triangular numbers below an integer n ⩾ 2, so triangular
44:                                           numbers are more sparse than prime numbers. In 2008 the author made the following
45:                                           conjecture.
46:                                           Conjecture 1.2 (Sun [22]). (i) Each natural number n 6= 216 can be written in the form
47:                                           p + Tx with x ∈ N, where p is zero or a prime.
48:                                             (ii) Any odd integer greater than 3 can be written in the form p + x(x + 1), where p is
49:                                           a prime and x is positive integer.
50: 
51:                                             Key words and phrases. Mixed sum, prime, Fibonacci number, linear recurrence, representation.
52:                                             2000 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 11P32; Secondary 11A41, 11B37, 11B39, 11B75,
53:                                           11Y99.
54:                                             Research supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (grant 10871087).
55:                                                                                               1
56: 
60: 10 and 1012 respectively. The author [23] would like to offer 1000 US dollars for the first
61:     10
62: 
63: positive solutions to both (i) and (ii), and $200 for the first explicit counterexample to (i)
64: or (ii).
65:    Powers of two are even much more sparse than triangular numbers. In a letter to
66: Goldbach, Euler posed the problem whether any odd integer n > 1 can be expressed in
67: the form p+2a , where p is a prime and a ∈ N. This question was reformulated by Polignac
68: in 1849. By introducing covers of the integers by residue classes, Erdős [4] showed that
69: there exists an infinite arithmetic progression of positive odd integers no term of which is
70: of the form p + 2a . (See also Nathanson [14, pp. 204-208].) On the basis of the work of
71: Cohen and Selfridge [2], the author [17] proved that if
72:                        x ≡ 47867742232066880047611079 (mod M)
73: with
141: [25] were able to construct a residue class containing no integers of the form pa + F3n /2
142: with p a prime and a, n ∈ N. Note that un = F3n /2 is just half of an even Fibonacci
143: number; also u0 = 0, u1 = 1, and un+1 = 4un + un−1 for n = 1, 2, 3, . . ..
144:   On December 23, 2008 the author [19] formulated the following conjecture.
145: Conjecture 1.5 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Fibonacci Numbers). Any integer
146: n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime and two positive Fibonacci numbers. We
147: can require further that one of the two Fibonacci numbers is odd.
148: Remark 1.6. For a large integer n, there are about log n/ log ϕ Fibonacci numbers below
149: n but there are about n/ log n primes below n. So, Fibonacci numbers are much more
150: sparse than prime numbers and hence the above conjecture looks more difficult than the
151: Goldbach conjecture. D. McNeil (cf. [12, 13]) has verified Conjecture 1.5 up to 1014 . The
152: author (cf. [23]) would like to offer 5000 US dollars for the first positive solution published
153: in a well-known mathematical journal and $250 for the first explicit counterexample which
154: can be rechecked by the author via computer. Note that Conjecture 1.5 implies that for
155: any odd prime p we can find an odd prime q < p such that p − q can be written as the
156: sum of two odd Fibonacci numbers.

161:                 P0 = 0, P1 = 1, and Pn+1 = 2Pn + Pn−1 for n = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
162: It is well known that
163:                          1            √ n     √ n

171:    On Jan. 10, 2009, the author [20] posed the following conjecture which is an analogue
172: of Conjecture 1.5.
173: Conjecture 1.7 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Pell Numbers). Any integer n > 5
174: can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a Pell number and twice a Pell number. We
175: can require further that the two Pell numbers are positive.
176: Remark 1.8. D. McNeil (cf. [23]) has verified Conjecture 1.7 up to 5 × 1013 and found
177: no counterexample. The author (cf. [23]) would like to offer 1000 US dollars for the first
178: positive solution published in a well-known mathematical journal and $100 for the first
179: explicit counterexample which can be rechecked by the author via computer.
180:   Soon after he learned Conjecture 1.7 from the author, Qing-Hu Hou (Nankai University)
181: observed (without proof) that all the sums Ps +2Pt (s, t = 1, 2, 3, . . .) are distinct. Clearly
182: Hou’s observation follows from our following theorem.
183: Theorem 1.9. Let a > 1 be an integer, and set
184:                   u0 = 0, u1 = 1, and ui+1 = aui + ui−1 for i = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
185: Then no integer x can be written as um + aun (with m ∈ N and n ∈ Z+ ) in at least two
186: ways, except in the case a = 2 and x = u0 + au2 = u2 + au1 = 4.
187: Remark 1.10. Note that if n ∈ Z+ then un+1 + au0 = aun + un−1.
188: Corollary 1.11. Let k, l, m, n ∈ Z+ . Then Pk + 2Pl = Pm + 2Pn if and only if k = m
189: and l = n.
190: Remark 1.12. In view of Corollary 1.11, we can assign an ordered pair hm, ni ∈ Z+ ×Z+
192: called a Sidon sequence if all the sums of pairs, ai + aj , are all distinct. An unsolved
193: problem of Erdős (cf. [7, p. 403]) asks for a polynomial P (x) ∈ Z[x] such that all the
194: sums P (m) + P (n) (0 ⩽ m < n) are distinct.
195:    Motivated by Conjecture 1.5 and its variants, Qing-Hu Hou and Jiang Zeng (University
196: of Lyon-I) formulated the following conjecture during their visit to the author in Jan. 2009.
197: Conjecture 1.13 (Hou and Zeng [9]). Any integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of
198: an odd prime, a positive Fibonacci number and a Catalan number.

204:                        1    2n       2n         2n
205:                 Cn =             =        −           (n ∈ N),
206:                       n+1 n           n        n+1
207: which play important roles in combinatorics (see, e.g., Stanley [16, Chapter 6]). They are
212:                                   k=0
213:                                   √
214: By Stirling’s formula, Cn ∼ 4n /(n nπ) as n → +∞. D. McNeil [13] has verified Conjec-
215: ture 1.13 up to 3 × 1013 and found no counterexample. Hou and Zeng would like to offer
216: 1000 US dollars for the first positive solution published in a well-known mathematical
217: journal and $200 for the first explicit counterexample which can be rechecked by them
218: via computer. Note that 3627586 cannot be written in the form p + 2Fs + Ct with p a
219: prime and s, t ∈ N.
220:   The Lucas sequence {Ln }n⩾0 is defined as follows.
221:                 L0 = 2, L1 = 1, and Ln+1 = Ln + Ln−1 (n = 1, 2, 3, . . .).
222: It is known that
223:                                                  √ n     √ n

225:                      Ln = 2Fn+1 − Fn =                +

228:    On Jan. 16, 2009 the author (cf. [20]) made the following conjecture which is similar
229: to Conjecture 1.13.
230: Conjecture 1.15. Each integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a
231: Lucas number and a Catalan number.
232: Remark 1.16. D. McNeil [13] has verified Conjecture 1.15 up to 1013 and found no
233: counterexample. Note that 1389082 cannot be written in the form p + 2Ls + Ct with p a
234: prime and s, t ∈ N.
235:    Recall that there are infinitely many positive odd integers not of the form p + 2a + 2b
236: with p a prime and a, b ∈ Z+ . However, Crocker’s trick in his proof of this result does
237: not work for the form p + 2a + 2b + 2c with p a prime and a, b, c ∈ Z+ . So we pose the
238: following conjecture which has been verified for odd integers below 1013 .
239: Conjecture 1.17 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Powers of Two). Any odd integer
240: greater than 3 can be written as the sum of an odd prime and at most three positive powers
241: of two.
242:   We are going to prove Theorems 1.3 and 1.9 in the next section. Section 3 is devoted
243: to our discussion of Conjecture 1.5 and its variants.

408:   Here is another variant of Conjecture 1.5 made by the author (cf. [19, 21]).
409: Conjecture 3.3. (i) Any integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, an
410: odd Lucas number and a positive Lucas number. For k = 2, 3 we can write any integer
411: n > 4 in the form p + Ls + Lkt , where p is an odd prime, s, t ⩾ 0, and Ls or Lt is odd.
412:   (ii) Each integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a positive Fibonacci
413: number and twice a positive Fibonacci number (or half of a positive Fibonacci number).
414: We can also represent any integer n > 4 as the sum of an odd prime, twice a positive
415: Fibonacci number, and the square of a positive Fibonacci number.
416:   (iii) Any integer n > 4 can be written in the form p + Fs + Lt with p an odd prime,
417: s > 0, and Fs or Lt odd.
418: Remark 3.4. The author verified Conjectures 3.1 and 3.3 for n ⩽ 3 × 107 . Qing-Hu Hou
419: found that 17540144 cannot be written as the sum of a prime, a Lucas number and the
420: fourth power of a Lucas number. McNeil (cf. [12]) has verified the first assertions in parts
421: (i) and (ii) of Conjectures 3.1 and 3.3 up to 1012 . He (cf. [13]) has also verified part (iii)
422: of Conjecture 3.3 up to 1013 , and found that 36930553345551 cannot be written as the
423: sum of a prime, a Fibonacci number and an even Lucas number.
424:   What about the representations n = p+Ps +kPt with k ∈ {1, 3, 4} related to Conjecture
425: 1.7? Note that 2176 cannot be written as the sum of a prime and two Pell numbers. McNeil
426: [13] found that 393185153350 cannot be written as the sum of a prime, a Pell number
427: and three times a Pell number, and the smallest integer greater than 7 not representable
428: as the sum of a prime, a Pell number and four times a Pell number is
429:                                 872377759846 ≈ 8.7 × 1011 .
430: The companion Pell sequence {Qn }n⩾0 is defined by
431:                   Q0 = Q1 = 2 and Qn+1 = 2Qn + Qn−1 (n = 1, 2, 3, . . .).
432: McNeil [13] found that the smallest integer greater than 5 not representable as the sum
433: of a prime, a Pell number and a companion Pell number is 169421772576.
434:    McNeil’s counterexamples seem to suggest that Conjecture 1.7 might also have large
435: counterexamples. However, in the author’s opinion, the large counterexamples to the
436: representations n = p + Ps + 3Pt and n = p + Ps + 4Pt hint that they are very close to the
437: “truth” (Conjecture 1.7). Corollary 1.11 is also a good evidence to support Conjecture
438: 1.7. To expel suspicion, the author has investigated the behavior of the representation
439: function
440:             r(n) = |{hp, s, ti : p + Ps + 2Pt = n with p a prime and s, t ⩾ 0}|.
