22:                                                                                    1. Introduction
23:                                             Let us first recall the famous Goldbach conjecture in additive number theory.
24:                                           Conjecture 1.1 (Goldbach’s Conjecture). Any even integer n ⩾ 4 can be written as the
25:                                           sum of two primes.
26:                                             The number of primes not exceeding n ⩾ 2 is approximately n/ log n by the prime
27:                                           number theorem. Hardy and Littlewood conjectured that the number of ways to write an
28:                                           even integer n ⩾ 4 as the sum of two primes is given asymptotically by
29:                                                                                               
30:                                                                             cn Y            1
31:                                                                                      1+          ,
32:                                                                           log2 n          p−2
33:                                                                                         p|n
34: 
35:                                           where c = 2 p (1 − (p − 1)−2 ) = 1.3203 · · · is a constant and p runs over odd primes. (Cf.
36:                                                        Q
37: 
38:                                           [7, pp. 159-164].)
39:                                              Goldbach’s conjecture remains open, and the best result in this direction is Chen’s
40:                                           theorem (cf. [1]): Each large even integer can be written as the sum of a prime and a
41:                                           product of at most two primes.
42:                                              Those integers Tx√= x(x+1)/2 with x ∈ N = {0, 1, 2, . . .} are called triangular numbers.
43:                                           There are less than 2n positive triangular numbers below an integer n ⩾ 2, so triangular
44:                                           numbers are more sparse than prime numbers. In 2008 the author made the following
45:                                           conjecture.
46:                                           Conjecture 1.2 (Sun [22]). (i) Each natural number n 6= 216 can be written in the form
47:                                           p + Tx with x ∈ N, where p is zero or a prime.
48:                                             (ii) Any odd integer greater than 3 can be written in the form p + x(x + 1), where p is
49:                                           a prime and x is positive integer.
50: 
51:                                             Key words and phrases. Mixed sum, prime, Fibonacci number, linear recurrence, representation.
52:                                             2000 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 11P32; Secondary 11A41, 11B37, 11B39, 11B75,
53:                                           11Y99.
54:                                             Research supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (grant 10871087).
55:                                                                                               1
56: 
60: 10 and 1012 respectively. The author [23] would like to offer 1000 US dollars for the first
61:     10
62: 
63: positive solutions to both (i) and (ii), and $200 for the first explicit counterexample to (i)
64: or (ii).
65:    Powers of two are even much more sparse than triangular numbers. In a letter to
66: Goldbach, Euler posed the problem whether any odd integer n > 1 can be expressed in
67: the form p+2a , where p is a prime and a ∈ N. This question was reformulated by Polignac
68: in 1849. By introducing covers of the integers by residue classes, Erdős [4] showed that
69: there exists an infinite arithmetic progression of positive odd integers no term of which is
70: of the form p + 2a . (See also Nathanson [14, pp. 204-208].) On the basis of the work of
71: Cohen and Selfridge [2], the author [17] proved that if
72:                        x ≡ 47867742232066880047611079 (mod M)
73: with
141: [25] were able to construct a residue class containing no integers of the form pa + F3n /2
142: with p a prime and a, n ∈ N. Note that un = F3n /2 is just half of an even Fibonacci
143: number; also u0 = 0, u1 = 1, and un+1 = 4un + un−1 for n = 1, 2, 3, . . ..
144:   On December 23, 2008 the author [19] formulated the following conjecture.
145: Conjecture 1.5 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Fibonacci Numbers). Any integer
146: n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime and two positive Fibonacci numbers. We
147: can require further that one of the two Fibonacci numbers is odd.
148: Remark 1.6. For a large integer n, there are about log n/ log ϕ Fibonacci numbers below
149: n but there are about n/ log n primes below n. So, Fibonacci numbers are much more
150: sparse than prime numbers and hence the above conjecture looks more difficult than the
151: Goldbach conjecture. D. McNeil (cf. [12, 13]) has verified Conjecture 1.5 up to 1014 . The
152: author (cf. [23]) would like to offer 5000 US dollars for the first positive solution published
153: in a well-known mathematical journal and $250 for the first explicit counterexample which
154: can be rechecked by the author via computer. Note that Conjecture 1.5 implies that for
155: any odd prime p we can find an odd prime q < p such that p − q can be written as the
156: sum of two odd Fibonacci numbers.

160:     Recall that the Pell sequence {Pn }n⩾0 is defined as follows.
161:                 P0 = 0, P1 = 1, and Pn+1 = 2Pn + Pn−1 for n = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
162: It is well known that
163:                          1            √ n     √ n
164:                   Pn = √ (1 + 2) − (1 − 2)            for all n ∈ N.
168:                                       (1 + 2)n
169:                             Pn ∼          √     (n → +∞).
170:                                          2 2
171:    On Jan. 10, 2009, the author [20] posed the following conjecture which is an analogue
172: of Conjecture 1.5.
173: Conjecture 1.7 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Pell Numbers). Any integer n > 5
174: can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a Pell number and twice a Pell number. We
175: can require further that the two Pell numbers are positive.
176: Remark 1.8. D. McNeil (cf. [23]) has verified Conjecture 1.7 up to 5 × 1013 and found
177: no counterexample. The author (cf. [23]) would like to offer 1000 US dollars for the first
178: positive solution published in a well-known mathematical journal and $100 for the first
179: explicit counterexample which can be rechecked by the author via computer.
180:   Soon after he learned Conjecture 1.7 from the author, Qing-Hu Hou (Nankai University)
181: observed (without proof) that all the sums Ps +2Pt (s, t = 1, 2, 3, . . .) are distinct. Clearly
182: Hou’s observation follows from our following theorem.
183: Theorem 1.9. Let a > 1 be an integer, and set
184:                   u0 = 0, u1 = 1, and ui+1 = aui + ui−1 for i = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
185: Then no integer x can be written as um + aun (with m ∈ N and n ∈ Z+ ) in at least two
186: ways, except in the case a = 2 and x = u0 + au2 = u2 + au1 = 4.
187: Remark 1.10. Note that if n ∈ Z+ then un+1 + au0 = aun + un−1.
188: Corollary 1.11. Let k, l, m, n ∈ Z+ . Then Pk + 2Pl = Pm + 2Pn if and only if k = m
189: and l = n.
190: Remark 1.12. In view of Corollary 1.11, we can assign an ordered pair hm, ni ∈ Z+ ×Z+
192: called a Sidon sequence if all the sums of pairs, ai + aj , are all distinct. An unsolved
193: problem of Erdős (cf. [7, p. 403]) asks for a polynomial P (x) ∈ Z[x] such that all the
194: sums P (m) + P (n) (0 ⩽ m < n) are distinct.
195:    Motivated by Conjecture 1.5 and its variants, Qing-Hu Hou and Jiang Zeng (University
196: of Lyon-I) formulated the following conjecture during their visit to the author in Jan. 2009.
197: Conjecture 1.13 (Hou and Zeng [9]). Any integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of
198: an odd prime, a positive Fibonacci number and a Catalan number.
199: 
200:                          MIXED SUMS OF PRIMES AND OTHER TERMS                                  5
201: 
202: Remark 1.14. Catalan numbers are integers of the form
203:                                                
204:                        1    2n       2n         2n
205:                 Cn =             =        −           (n ∈ N),
206:                       n+1 n           n        n+1
207: which play important roles in combinatorics (see, e.g., Stanley [16, Chapter 6]). They are
212:                                 n
213:                                   √
214: By Stirling’s formula, Cn ∼ 4 /(n nπ) as n → +∞. D. McNeil [13] has verified Conjec-
215: ture 1.13 up to 3 × 1013 and found no counterexample. Hou and Zeng would like to offer
216: 1000 US dollars for the first positive solution published in a well-known mathematical
217: journal and $200 for the first explicit counterexample which can be rechecked by them
218: via computer. Note that 3627586 cannot be written in the form p + 2Fs + Ct with p a
219: prime and s, t ∈ N.
220:   The Lucas sequence {Ln }n⩾0 is defined as follows.
221:                  L0 = 2, L1 = 1, and Ln+1 = Ln + Ln−1 (n = 1, 2, 3, . . .).
222: It is known that
223:                                                   √ n     √ n
224:                                                 1+ 5      1− 5
225:                       Ln = 2Fn+1 − Fn =                +
226:                                                   2         2
227: for every n = 0, 1, 2, 3, . . ..
228:    On Jan. 16, 2009 the author (cf. [20]) made the following conjecture which is similar
229: to Conjecture 1.13.
230: Conjecture 1.15. Each integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a
231: Lucas number and a Catalan number.
232: Remark 1.16. D. McNeil [13] has verified Conjecture 1.15 up to 1013 and found no
233: counterexample. Note that 1389082 cannot be written in the form p + 2Ls + Ct with p a
234: prime and s, t ∈ N.
235:   Recall that there are infinitely many positive odd integers not of the form p + 2a + 2b
236: with p a prime and a, b ∈ Z+ . However, Crocker’s trick in his proof of this result does not
237: work for the form p + 2a + k2b with p a prime and a, b ∈ Z+ , where k is an odd integer
238: greater than one. On Jan. 21, 2009 the author (cf. [20]) made the following conjecture.
239: Conjecture 1.17 (Conjecture on Sums of Primes and Powers of Two). Any odd integer
240: greater than 8 can be written as the sum of an odd prime and three positive powers of two.
241: Moreover, we can write any odd integer n > 10 in the form p+2a +3×2b = p+2a +2b +2b+1
242: with p a prime and a, b ∈ Z+ .
243: Remark 1.18. The author verified Conjecture 1.17 for odd integers below 107 . Later, on
244: the request of the author, Qing-Hu Hou and Charles Greathouse continued the verification
245: for odd integers below 2×108 and 1010 respectively. Note that if k > 61 is odd then 2k+127
246: cannot be written in the form p + 2a + k2b with p an odd prime and a, b ∈ Z+ since
247: 3 + 2 + k22 > 2k + 127 and 127 is not of the form p + 2a . For k ∈ {3, 5, . . . , 61} \ {47, 51},
248: 
249: 6                                             ZHI-WEI SUN
250: 
251: the author (cf. [19]) checked odd integers below 108 and found no odd integer n > 2k + 3
252: not of the form p + 2a + k2b with p an odd prime and a, b ∈ Z+ .
253:   We are going to prove Theorems 1.3 and 1.9 in the next section. Section 3 is devoted
254: to our discussion of Conjecture 1.5 and its variants.

374:                   3. Discussion of Conjecture 1.5 and its variants
375:    Concerning Conjecture 1.5, we mention that there are very few natural numbers not
376: representable as the sum of a prime p ≡ 5 (mod 6) and two Fibonacci numbers. Bjorn
377: Poonen (MIT) informed the author that by a heuristic argument there should be infinitely
378: many positive integers not in the form p + Fs + Ft if we require that the prime p lies in
379: a fixed residue classe with modulus greater than one. McNeil [11, 13] made a computer
380: search to find natural numbers not representable as the sum of a prime p ≡ 5 (mod 6), an
381: odd Fibonacci number and a positive Fibonacci number; he found that there are totally
382: 729 such numbers in the interval [0, 1014 ], 277 of which (such as 857530546) even cannot
383: be written as the sum of a prime p ≡ 5 (mod 6) and two Fibonacci numbers.
384:    In 2008 the author (cf. [19, 20]) also made the following conjecture which is similar to
385: Conjecture 1.5.
386: Conjecture 3.1. (i) Any integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a
387: positive Fibonacci number and the square of a positive Fibonacci number. We can require
388: further that one of the two Fibonacci numbers is odd.
389:   (ii) Each integer n > 4 can be written as the sum of an odd prime, a positive Fibonacci
390: number and the cube of a positive Fibonacci number. We can require further that one of
391: the two Fibonacci numbers is odd.
392: Remark 3.2. Note that 900068 cannot be written as the sum of a prime, a Fibonacci
393: number and the fourth power of a Fibonacci number. Also,

16:                                                 and more general “d-refined” enumerations of ASMs according to the first d rows.
17:                                                 For the doubly-refined case of d = 2, we derive a system of linear equations satisfied
18:                                                 by the doubly-refined enumeration numbers An,i,j that enumerate such matrices. We
19:                                                 give a conjectural explicit formula for An,i,j and formulate several other conjectures
20:                                                 about the sufficiency of the linear equations to determine the An,i,j ’s and about an
21:                                                 extension of the linear equations to the general d-refined enumerations.
22: 
23: 
24:                                                 1     Introduction
25:                                                 1.1     Alternating sign matrices
26:                                                 An alternating sign matrix (ASM) of order n is an n × n matrix with entries
27:                                                 in {0, −1, 1} such that in every row and every column, the sum of the entries is
28:                                                 1 and the non-zero numbers appear with alternating signs; see Fig. 1(a) for an
29:                                                 example with n = 5. Alternating sign matrices were first defined and studied in
45:            Robbins and Rumsey were also interested in the enumeration of ASMs. To-
46:        gether with William Mills, they denoted by An the total number of ASMs of order
47:        n, and by An,k for 1 ≤ k ≤ n the number of ASMs with a 1 in position k of row 1,
48:        and conjectured [7] two important enumeration identities, nowadays known as the
49:        alternating sign matrix theorem and the refined alternating sign matrix
50:        theorem, which state that
51:                                    n−1
52:                                    Y     (3j + 1)!
53:                          An =                            and                                (1)
54:                                           (n + j)!
55:                                    j=0
56:                                                         n−2
57:                                   n + k − 2 (2n − k − 1)! Y (3j + 1)!
58:                         An,k   =                                       .                    (2)
59:                                     k−1       (n − k)!        (n + j)!
60:                                                                j=0
61: 
62:        The attempt to prove these celebrated conjectures and subsequent developments
63:        have led to a rich combinatorial and algebraic theory relating ASMs to other com-
64:        binatorial objects such as domino tilings and plane partitions, to representation
65:        theory and to the six-vertex model and other related lattice models in statistical
66:        physics. Eqs. (1) and (2) were proved by Zeilberger [11, 12], who in the refined case
67:        of (2) (which contains (1) as a special case, since An,1 = An−1 , and took longer to
68:        prove) used the square-ice techniques introduced by Kuperberg [6] in his simplified
69:        proof of (1). An alternative proof of (2) was found by Fischer [3, 4], and another
70:        proof based on some variations on the Kuperberg-Zeilberger approach was found by
71:        Stroganov [10].
72:            Our goal in this paper is to extend these enumeration results to more refined
73:        enumerations of ASMs. In the simplest case, which we call the doubly-refined
74:        enumeration, we will define numbers An,i,j that enumerate ASMs based on their
75:        first two rows rather than the first row1 . We will present a conjectural explicit
76:        formula for An,i,j that turns out to be considerably more complicated than the
77:        product formulas (1) and (2). While proving this formula may look challenging,
78:        we take a significant first step by deriving and proving a system of linear equations
79:        satisfied (for each fixed n) by the numbers (An,i,j )i,j . Assuming a natural conjecture
80:        that we will formulate concerning the sufficiency of these equations to determine the
81:        An,i,j ’s, proving the explicit formula is reduced to the (possibly quite complicated,
82:        but essentially mechanical) verification that the expression given by that formula
83:        satisfies the system of linear equations.
84:    1
85:      There is a different doubly-refined enumeration based on the first and last rows, studied e.g. in
86: [5, 10], which we will not discuss in this paper.
87: 
88: 
89: 
91: 
92:     One can also consider the triply-refined, quadruply-refined, and more generally
93: the d-refined enumeration of ASMs based on their first d rows. Our main result
94: concerning the system of linear equations extends conjecturally (and empirically for
95: d = 3 and small values of n) to this generality. We believe our techniques are also
96: relevant for attacking this more general problem (they are in fact an extension of the
97: techniques used in [4] to attack the original refined case of d = 1). The existence of
98: such detailed equations governing the d-refined enumeration is surprising and may
99: offer a useful tool for attacking important open problems such as the problem of
100: the limit shape of large random ASMs that was partially analyzed in [2].
101:

413: 1.4      The d-refined enumeration
414: The linear equations in Theorem 1 extend conjecturally to the d-refined enumeration
415: of ASMs, for any d ≥ 1. Once again, the formulation in terms of monotone triangles
416: turns out to be the most convenient.

418: 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < . . . < id ≤ n, let An,i1 ,i2 ,...,id denote the number of monotone tri-
419: angles of order n − d whose bottom row consists of the numbers in {1, 2, . . . , n} \
420: {i1 , i2 , . . . , id } arranged in increasing order (if d = n then we set An,1,2,...,n = 1).
421: Then (An,i1 ,...,id )i1 <...<id can be extended into an array (Ân,i1 ,...,id )1≤i1 ,...,id ≤n such

438: 1.5      A family of polynomial expansions

440: a corresponding conjectural extension to the d-refined enumeration for d ≥ 3) that
441: is more conceptual than the linear equations (8) described above, and whose proof
442: will take up the main part of the paper. At the same time we also outline how this
443: result will be used to derive the main results formulated above.
444:     To introduce this result, we consider the function

447: 
448: defined in [3], that counts the number of monotone triangles with bottom row
449: (k1 , . . . , kn ). This function is a polynomial with many useful symmetries. In [4]
450: it was proved that the one-variable polynomial defined as a specialization of αn by
493: equations (8) will follow relatively easily from a combination of this expansion with
494: the symmetry property of Gn (x, y). Along the way we gain enough information
495: about the Ân,i,j ’s to also deduce Theorem 2.
496:     Theorem 6 and eq. (13) also extend conjecturally to the d-refined enumerations
497: for d ≥ 3, in the following form.

24:                                                 1     Introduction
25:                                                 1.1     Alternating sign matrices
26:                                                 An alternating sign matrix (ASM) of order n is an n × n matrix with entries
27:                                                 in {0, −1, 1} such that in every row and every column, the sum of the entries is
28:                                                 1 and the non-zero numbers appear with alternating signs; see Fig. 1(a) for an
29:                                                 example with n = 5. Alternating sign matrices were first defined and studied in
45:            Robbins and Rumsey were also interested in the enumeration of ASMs. To-
46:        gether with William Mills, they denoted by An the total number of ASMs of order
47:        n, and by An,k for 1 ≤ k ≤ n the number of ASMs with a 1 in position k of row 1,
48:        and conjectured [9] two important enumeration identities, nowadays known as the
49:        alternating sign matrix theorem and the refined alternating sign matrix
50:        theorem, which state that
51:                                    n−1
52:                                    Y     (3j + 1)!
53:                          An =                            and                                (1)
54:                                           (n + j)!
55:                                    j=0
56:                                                         n−2
57:                                   n + k − 2 (2n − k − 1)! Y (3j + 1)!
58:                         An,k   =                                       .                    (2)
59:                                     k−1       (n − k)!        (n + j)!
60:                                                                j=0
61: 
62:        The attempt to prove these celebrated conjectures and subsequent developments
63:        have led to a rich combinatorial and algebraic theory relating ASMs to other com-
64:        binatorial objects such as domino tilings and plane partitions, to representation
65:        theory and to the six-vertex model and other related lattice models in statistical
66:        physics. Eqs. (1) and (2) were proved by Zeilberger [13, 14], who in the refined case
67:        of (2) (which contains (1) as a special case, since An,1 = An−1 , and took longer to
68:        prove) used the square-ice techniques introduced by Kuperberg [8] in his simplified
69:        proof of (1). An alternative proof of (2) using entirely different methods was found
70:        by Fischer [5, 6]. Other variations on the Kuperberg-Zeilberger approach lead to
71:        different derivations of (1) by Colomo and Pronko [2] and of (2) by Stroganov [12].
72:            Our goal in this paper is to extend these enumeration results to more refined
73:        enumerations of ASMs. In the simplest case, which we call the doubly-refined
74:        enumeration, we will define numbers An,i,j that enumerate ASMs based on their
75:        first two rows rather than the first row1 . We will present a conjectural explicit
76:        formula for An,i,j that turns out to be considerably more complicated than the
77:        product formulas (1) and (2). While proving this formula may look challenging,
78:        we take a significant first step by deriving and proving a system of linear equations
79:        satisfied (for each fixed n) by the numbers (An,i,j )i,j . Assuming a natural conjecture
80:        that we will formulate concerning the sufficiency of these equations to determine the
81:        An,i,j ’s, proving the explicit formula is reduced to the (possibly quite complicated,
82:        but essentially mechanical) verification that the expression given by that formula
83:        satisfies the system of linear equations.
84:    1
85:      There is a different doubly-refined enumeration based on the first and last rows, studied e.g. in
86: [3, 7, 12], which we will not discuss in this paper.
87: 
88: 
89:                                                      2
90: 
91:     One can also consider the triply-refined, quadruply-refined, and more generally
92: the d-refined enumeration of ASMs based on their first d rows. Our main result
93: concerning the system of linear equations extends conjecturally (and empirically for
94: d = 3 and small values of n) to this generality. We believe our techniques are also
95: relevant for attacking this more general problem (they are in fact an extension of the
96: techniques used in [6] to attack the original refined case of d = 1). The existence of
97: such detailed equations governing the d-refined enumeration is surprising and may
98: offer a useful tool for attacking important open problems such as the problem of the
99: limit shape of large random ASMs, for which a conjectural formula is given in [4].
100: 
101: 1.2     Monotone triangles
102: To formulate our results precisely, we need first to define the doubly-refined enu-
103: meration numbers An,i,j . This is more conveniently done in terms of a family of
104: objects called monotone triangles that are related to ASMs. A monotone triangle
105: of order n is a triangular array (ti,j )1≤i≤n,1≤j≤i of integers satisfying the inequalities
106:           ti,j < ti,j+1 ,   ti,j ≤ ti−1,j ≤ ti,j+1     (2 ≤ i ≤ n, 1 ≤ j ≤ i − 1).
107: In other words, in the coordinate system traditionally used to draw them, monotone
108: triangles have strictly increasing rows and weakly increasing northwest-southeast
109: and southwest-northeast diagonals; see Fig. 1(b).
313: together with the “boundary” information (4), is sufficient to determine them.
314: Strong numerical evidence suggests that the answer is positive.
315: 
316: Conjecture 3 (Sufficiency). The equations (8), together with the near-symmetry
317: equations (10), (11) and the special values (4), determine the numbers Ân,i,j uniquely.

321: of Cramer’s rule it should be possible to write a formula that expresses An,i,j as
322: a quotient of determinants. We also found the following formula, which is more
323: explicit than a determinantal formula but still considerably more complicated than
324: the formulas (1), (2).

412: 1.4      The d-refined enumeration
413: The linear equations in Theorem 1 extend conjecturally to the d-refined enumeration
414: of ASMs, for any d ≥ 1. Once again, the formulation in terms of monotone triangles
415: turns out to be the most convenient.
416: Conjecture 5 (Linear equations for the d-refined case). For 1 ≤ d < n and
417: 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < . . . < id ≤ n, let An,i1 ,i2 ,...,id denote the number of monotone tri-
418: angles of order n − d whose bottom row consists of the numbers in {1, 2, . . . , n} \
419: {i1 , i2 , . . . , id } arranged in increasing order (if d = n then we set An,1,2,...,n = 1).
420: Then (An,i1 ,...,id )i1 <...<id can be extended into an array (Ân,i1 ,...,id )1≤i1 ,...,id ≤n such
421: that the equations

437: 1.5      A family of polynomial expansions
438: We now formulate another result on the doubly-refined enumeration numbers (with
439: a corresponding conjectural extension to the d-refined enumeration for d ≥ 3) that
440: is more conceptual than the linear equations (8) described above, and whose proof
441: will take up the main part of the paper. At the same time we also outline how this
442: result will be used to derive the main results formulated above.
443:     To introduce this result, we consider the function
444: 
445:                                              αn (k1 , k2 , . . . , kn ),
446: 
447: defined in [5], that counts the number of monotone triangles with bottom row
448: (k1 , . . . , kn ). This function is a polynomial with many useful symmetries. In [6]
449: it was proved that the one-variable polynomial defined as a specialization of αn by
492: equations (8) will follow relatively easily from a combination of this expansion with
493: the symmetry property of Gn (x, y). Along the way we gain enough information
494: about the Ân,i,j ’s to also deduce Theorem 2.
495:     Theorem 6 and eq. (13) also extend conjecturally to the d-refined enumerations
496: for d ≥ 3, in the following form.
497: Conjecture 7. For each d ≥ 1, define the polynomials Gn (x1 , . . . , xd ) (n ≥ d) by
498: 
499: Gn (x1 , . . . , xd ) = αn (1, 2, . . . , n−d, n−d+1+x1 , n−d+2+x2 , . . . , n−1+xd−1 , n+xd ).
500:

20: 
21: 
22:                                           1        Introduction
23:                                           A topological graph is a graph drawn in the plane with points as vertices and edges as non-self-
24:                                           intersecting arcs connecting its vertices. The arcs are allowed to intersect, but they may not
25:                                           pass through vertices except for their endpoints. Furthermore, the edges are not allowed to have
26:                                           tangencies, i.e., if two edges share an interior point, then they must properly cross at that point.
27:                                           We only consider graphs without parallel edges or self-loops. A topological graph is simple if every
28:                                           pair of its edges intersect at most once. If the edges are drawn as straight-line segments, then the
34:                                           number of edges in a topological (and geometric) graph with a forbidden substructure has been a
35:                                           classic problem in extremal topological graph theory (see [1], [2], [11], [5], [16], [10], [15], [14], [17]).
36:                                               Recently, Ackerman et al. [3] defined a natural (k, l)-grid to be a set of k pairwise disjoint edges
37:                                           that all cross another set of l pairwise disjoint edges. They conjectured
38: 
39:                                           Conjecture 1.1. Given fixed constants k, l ≥ 1 there exists another constant ck,l , such that any
40:                                           geometric graph on n vertices with no natural (k, l)-grid has at most ck,l n edges.
41: 
42:                                           They were able to show,

46:                                                   Courant Institute, New York and EPFL, Lausanne. Email: suk@cims.nyu.edu

64:   4. every edges in E1 is disjoint to every edge in E2 .
65: 
66: We conjecture
67: 
68: Conjecture 1.4. Given fixed constants k, l ≥ 1 there exists another constant ck,l , such that any
69: geometric graph on n vertices with no (k, l)-crossing family has at most ck,l n edges.
70: 
71:    It is not even known if all n-vertex geometric graphs with no k pairwise crossing edges has O(n)
72: edges. The best known bound is due to Valtr [18], who showed that this is at most O(n log n) for
73: every fixed k. We extend this result to (k, k)-crossing families by proving the following theorem.
74: 
75: Theorem 1.5. An n-vertex geometric graph with no (k, k)-crossing family has at most ck n log n
76: edges, where ck is a constant that depends only on k.
77: 
78: Moreover, we settle Conjecture 1.4 in the first nontrivial case:
79: 
80: Theorem 1.6. An n-vertex geometric graph with no (2, 1)-crossing family has at most O(n) = 22n
81: edges.
82: 
83: Note that in the previous theorem we do not strive for the best constant, since we see only an
84: improvement involving a tiring case analysis.
85:    Conjecture 1.4 is not true for topological graphs since Pach and Tóth [12] showed that the
86: complete graph can be drawn such that every pair of edges intersect once or twice. However for
87: simple topological graphs, we have the following
88: 
89: Theorem 1.7. An n-vertex simple topological graph with no (k, 1)-crossing family has at most
90: n(log n)O(log k) edges.

94: geometric subgraph. Therefore Theorem 1.6 can be also stated as ex((k, k)-crossingf amily, n) ≤

238: matching consisting of 4 pairwise disjoint edges, then |E(G)| ≤ 8.5n.
239: Proof of Theorem 1.6. We prove the theorem by trying to exhibit a counterexample to the theorem
240: having the minimum number of vertices. Let G = (V, E) be such an example on n vertices, i.e.
241: G is a geometric graph on n vertices which has more than 22n edges and G does not contain a
242: (2,1)-crossing family.
243:     Let M denote the maximum matching in G consisting of pairwise disjoint edges. By Theo-
244: rem 3.1, |M | ≥ 4. By an intersection of two edges we understand either their common endpoint or
245: a crossing. The following simple observation is crucial in the subsequent analysis.

26:                                           1       Introduction
27:                                           A topological graph is a graph drawn in the plane with points as vertices and edges as non-self-
28:                                           intersecting arcs connecting its vertices. The arcs are allowed to intersect, but they may not
29:                                           pass through vertices except for their endpoints. Furthermore, the edges are not allowed to have
30:                                           tangencies, i.e., if two edges share an interior point, then they must properly cross at that point.
55: devoted to understanding the clique and independence number of intersection graphs due to their
56: applications in VLSI design [8], map labeling [3], and elsewhere.
57:    Recently, Ackerman et al. [4] defined a natural (k, l)-grid to be a set of k pairwise disjoint edges
58: that all cross another set of l pairwise disjoint edges. They conjectured
59: Conjecture 1.1. Given fixed constants k, l ≥ 1 there exists another constant ck,l , such that any
60: geometric graph on n vertices with no natural (k, l)-grid has at most ck,l n edges.
61: They were able to show,
62: Theorem 1.2. [4] For fixed k, an n-vertex geometric graph with no natural (k, k)-grid has at most
63: O(n log2 n) edges.
64: Theorem 1.3. [4] An n-vertex geometric graph with no natural (2, 1)-grid has at most O(n) edges.
65: Theorem 1.4. [4] An n-vertex simple topological graph with no natural (k, k)-grid has at most
66: O(n log4k−6 n) edges.
67: As a dual version of the natural (k, l)-grid, we define a (k, l)-crossing family to be a pair of edge
68: subsets E1 , E2 such that
69: 
72:   3. the edges in E2 are pairwise crossing,
73:   4. every edge in E1 is disjoint to every edge in E2 .
74: 
75: We conjecture
76: Conjecture 1.5. Given fixed constants k, l ≥ 1 there exists another constant ck,l , such that any
77: geometric graph on n vertices with no (k, l)-crossing family has at most ck,l n edges.
78: It is not even known if all n-vertex geometric graphs with no k pairwise crossing edges has O(n)
79: edges. The best known bound is due to Valtr [22], who showed that this is at most O(n log n) for
80: every fixed k. We extend this result to (k, k)-crossing families by proving the following theorem.
81: Theorem 1.6. An n-vertex geometric graph with no (k, k)-crossing family has at most ck n log n
82: edges, where ck is a constant that depends only on k.
83:     Let F denote a geometric graph. We say that a geometric graph G contains F as a geometric
84: subgraph if G contains a subgraph F 0 isomorphic to F such that two edges in F 0 cross if and only
85: if the two corresponding edges cross in F .
86:     We define ex(F, n) to be the extremal function of F , i.e. the maximum number of edges a
99: 
100: Theorem 1.7. ex(F2 , n) = O((exL (F, 2n) + n) log n + ex(F, n))
101: 
102: Furthermore, we settle Conjecture 1.5 in the first nontrivial case.
103: 
104: Theorem 1.8. An n-vertex geometric graph with no (2, 1)-crossing family has at most O(n) edges.
105: 
106: Note that Conjecture 1.5 is not true for topological graphs since Pach and Tóth [16] showed that
107: the complete graph can be drawn such that every pair of edges intersect once or twice.
108:     Recall that F is a circle graph if F can be represented as the intersection graph of chords on a
109: circle. By combining Theorem 1.8 with results from [2], [4], and [20], we have the following.
110: 
111: Corollary 1.9. For any circle graph F on 3 vertices, every n-vertex geometric graph that does not
112: contain a matching whose intersection graph is F contains at most O(n) edges.
113: 
114: See Figure 1. We also conjecture the following.
115: 
116: Conjecture 1.10. For any circle graph F on k vertices, there exists a constant ck such that every
117: n-vertex geometric graph that does not contain a matching whose intersection graph is F , contains
118: at most ck n edges.
119: 
120: As pointed out by Klazar and Marcus [9], it is not hard to modify the proof of the Marcus-Tardos
121: Theorem [19] to show that Conjecture 1.10 is true when the vertices are in convex position.
122:    For simple topological graphs, we have the following
123: 
124: Theorem 1.11. An n-vertex simple topological graph with no (k, 1)-crossing family has at most
125: n(log n)O(log k) edges.
126: 
127:     The paper is organized as follows. Section 2 is devoted to the proof of Theorem 1.7. In Section
128: 3 we establish Theorem 1.8. Finally, the result of Theorem 1.11 about topological graphs is proved
129: in Section 4.

20:                                         in dependency on the surplus σ(F ). Lean clause-sets, defined as having no non-trivial autarkies, generalise minimally
21:                                         unsatisfiable clause-sets. For the surplus we have σ(F ) ≤ δ(F ) = c(F ) − n(F ), using the deficiency δ(F ) of clause-sets,
22:                                         the difference between the number of clauses and the number of variables. nM(k) is the k-th “non-Mersenne” number,
23:                                         skipping in the sequence of natural numbers all numbers of the form 2n − 1. We conjecture that µvd(F ) ≤ nM(δ(F ))
24:                                         is sharp for minimally unsatisfiable F (for every deficiency, and for arbitrarily large F ). As an application of the upper
25:                                         bound we obtain that clause-sets F violating µvd(F ) ≤ nM(σ(F )) must have a non-trivial autarky (so clauses can be
26:                                         removed satisfiability-equivalently by an assignment satisfying some clauses and not touching the other clauses). It is
27:                                         open whether such an autarky can be found in polynomial time.
28:                                         Keywords: conjunctive normal form, deficiency, minimal unsatisfiable clause-set, variable occurrences, lean clause-set,
29:                                         autarky, surplus, non-Mersenne numbers

84: nM(k) ≤ k+1+log2 (k). As an application we obtain (Corollary 4.2), that if a (multi-
85: )clause-set F has no variable occurring with degree at most δ(F ) + 1 + log2 (δ(F )),
86: then F has a non-trivial autarky. It is an open problem whether such an autarky can
87: be found in polynomial time (for arbitrary clause-sets F ); we conjecture (Conjecture
88: 4.3) that this is possible.
89: 
90: Related work This article appears to be the first systematic study of the problem
91: of minimum variable occurrences in minimally unsatisfiable clause-sets and gener-
92: alisations, in dependency on the deficiency, asking for the existence of a variable
93: occurring “infrequently” in general, or for extremal examples where all variables
94: occur not infrequently. The problem of maximum variable occurrences (asking for
95: the existence of a variable occurring frequently in general, or for extremal exam-
96: ples where all variables occur not frequently) in uniform (minimally) unsatisfiable
97: clause-sets, in dependency on the (constant) clause-length, has been studied in the
105: sets, proving Theorem 4.1. The immediate corollary of Theorem 4.1 is, that if
106: the asserted upper bound on the minimal variable degree is not fulfilled, then a
107: non-trivial autarky must exist (Corollary 4.2). In Subsection 4.3 the problem of
108: finding such autarky is discussed, with Conjecture 4.3 making precise our believe
109: that one can find such autarkies efficiently. In Section 5 we discuss the sharpness
110: of the bound, and the possibilities to generalise it further. Finally, in Section 6
111: 
112:                                           2
113: 
114: open problems are stated, culminating in the central Conjecture 6.1 about the
115: classification of unsatisfiable hitting clause-sets (or “disjoint tautologies” in the
116: terminology of DNFs).

119: 2     Preliminaries
120: We follow the general notations and definitions as outlined in [5], where also further
121: background on autarkies and minimal unsatisfiability can be found. We use N =
122: {1, 2, . . .} and N0 = N ∪ {0}.
123: 
124: 2.1    Clause-sets
415: 
416:    The quantities µvd(F ) and nM(σ(F )) (resp. nM(δ(F ))) are computable in poly-
417: nomial time, and so the applicability of Corollary 4.2 can be checked in polynomial
418: time. We conjecture that also “constructivisation” of Corollary 4.2 can be done in
419: polynomial time:
420: 
421: Conjecture 4.3 There is a poly-time algorithm for computing a non-trivial autarky
422: in case of µvd(F ) > nM(σ(F )) (or µvd(F ) > nM(δ(F ))) for matching-lean clause-
423: sets F .
424: 
425: See Subsection 4.3 for more discussion on Conjecture 4.3 (there also the remaining
426: details of Corollary 4.2 are proven).
427: 
428: 4.1    The special case of minimally unsatisfiable clause-sets
429: The main auxiliary lemma is the following statement, which receives its importance
430: from the fact that every minimally unsatisfiable clause-set can be saturated (this
431: method was first applied in [6]).
432: 
433: Lemma 4.4 Consider F ∈ SMUδ=k for k ∈ N and a variable v ∈ var(F ) realising
434: the minimal var-degree (i.e., vdF (v) = µvd(F )). Using m0 := ldF (v) and m1 :=
435: ldF (v) we have hv → εi ∗ F ∈ MUk−mε +1 for ε ∈ {0, 1}, where n(hv → εi ∗ F ) =
541: Subsection 4.2 there would be a variable v with vdF (v) ≤ nM(σ(F )).
542:     Now consider a matching-lean multi-clause-set F with n(F ) > 0, where Corol-
543: lary 4.2 is applicable (recall that we have σ(F ) ≥ 1), that is, we have µvd(F ) >
544: nM(σ(F )). So we know that F has a non-trivial autarky. Conjecture 4.3 states that
545: finding such a non-trivial autarky in this case can be done in polynomial time (recall
546: that finding a non-trivial autarky in general is NP-complete, which was shown in
547: [7]).
548:     The task of actually finding the autarky can be considered as finding a satisfy-
549: ing assignment for the following class MLCR ⊂ SAT ∩ MLEAN of satisfiable(!)
550: clause-sets F , obtained by considering all F [V ] for minimal sets of variables V with
551: δ(F [V ]) = σ(F ) (where “CR” stands for “critical”):
552: 
553: 
554: 
565: 
566: It is sufficient to find a non-trivial autarky for this class of satisfiable clause-sets.
567: 
568: Lemma 4.11 Conjecture 4.3 is equivalent to the statement, that finding a non-
569: trivial autarky for clause-sets in MLCR can be achieved in polynomial time.
570: 
571: At the time of writing this article, we are not aware of elements of MLCR with a
572: deficiency at least 2.
573: 
574: 
575: 5     On strengthening the bound
576: For a class C of clause-sets let µvd(C) be the supremum of µvd(F ) for F ∈ C
577: with n(F ) > 0. So by Theorem 4.5 we have µvd(MUδ=k ) ≤ nM(k) for all k ∈
578: N. The task of precisely determining µvd(MUδ=k ) for all k will be pursued in
592:     • It seems that this bound can not be generalised to lean clause-sets (as in
593:       Theorem 4.1).
594: 
595: Conjecture 5.1 For all k ∈ N we have µvd(MUδ=k ) ≥ nM(k) − 1.
596: 
597:     Now we consider the question whether the bound holds for a larger class of
598: clause-sets, that is, whether Theorem 4.1 can be generalised further, incorporating
599: non-lean clause-sets. We consider the large class MLEAN of matching lean clause-
600: sets, as introduced in [7], which is natural, since a basic property of F ∈ MU used
601: in the proof of Theorem 4.1 is δ(F ) ≥ 1 for F 6= ⊤, and this actually holds for
602: all F ∈ MLEAN . We will construct for arbitrary deficiency k ∈ N and K ∈ N
603: clause-sets F ∈ MLEAN of deficiency k where every variable occurs positively at
604: least K times. Thus neither the upper bound max(ldF (v), ldF (v)) ≤ f (δ(F )) nor
605: ldF (v)+ldF (v) = vdF (v) ≤ f (δ(F )) for some chosen variable v and for any function
658: the constructive aspects of the bound (i.e., what happens if the bound is violated).
659: There remain several open problems:
660: 
661:     1. Prove Conjecture 4.3, which says that such an autarky, which must exist if a
662:        clause-set does not fulfil the upper bound on the minimum variable degree of
663:        Theorem 4.1, can be found in polynomial time. See Subsection 4.3 for more
664:        information on this topic.
665:     2. Generalise Theorem 4.5 to clause-sets with non-boolean variables; see the
666:        discussion after Theorem 4.5.
667: 
668:                                           12
669: 
670:    3. See the remarks to Corollary 4.8 (an underlying question is to understand
671:       better the quantity “surplus”).
679: Especially the classification of unsatisfiable hitting clause-sets in dependency on the
680: deficiency seems very interesting (recall that a hitting clause-set F is defined by the
681: condition that every two clauses C, C ′ ∈ F , C 6= C ′ , clash in at least one variable,
682: that is |C ∩ C ′ | ≥ 1). The main conjecture is:
683: 
684: Conjecture 6.1 For every deficiency k ∈ N there are only finitely many isomor-
685: phism types of non-singular unsatisfiable hitting clause-sets.
686: 
687: For k ≤ 2 this conjecture follows from known results, while recently we were able
688: to prove it for k = 3.

14:                                                 monochromatic set congruent to X. This notion was introduced by
15:                                                 Erdős, Graham, Montgomery, Rothschild, Spencer and Straus, who
16:                                                 asked if a set is Ramsey if and only if it is spherical, meaning that it
17:                                                 lies on the surface of a sphere. This question (made into a conjecture
18:                                                 by Graham) has dominated subsequent work in Euclidean Ramsey
19:                                                 theory.
20:                                                     In this paper we introduce a new conjecture regarding which sets
21:                                                 are Ramsey; this is the first ever ‘rival’ conjecture to the conjec-
22:                                                 ture above. Calling a finite set transitive if its symmetry group acts
23:                                                 transitively—in other words, if all points of the set look the same—our
24:                                                 conjecture is that the Ramsey sets are precisely the transitive sets, to-
25:                                                 gether with their subsets. One appealing feature of this conjecture is
26:                                                 that it reduces (in one direction) to a purely combinatorial statement.
27:                                                 We give this statement as well as several other related conjectures.
28:                                                 We also prove the first non-trivial cases of the statement.
29:                                                     Curiously, it is far from obvious that our new conjecture is gen-
30:                                                 uinely different from the old. We show that they are indeed different
31:                                                 by proving that not every spherical set embeds in a transitive set.
32:                                                 This result may be of independent interest.
33:                                             ∗
34:                                                Department of Pure Mathematics and Mathematical Statistics, Centre for Mathemat-
35:                                          ical Sciences, Wilberforce Road, Cambridge CB3 0WB, England.
36:                                              †
37:                                                I.Leader@dpmms.cam.ac.uk
38:                                              ‡
39:                                                P.A.Russell@dpmms.cam.ac.uk
71: difficult) extension of the proof above that {0, 1, 2} is not Ramsey. So the
72: key question remaining was: is every spherical set Ramsey? It has been
73: widely believed for some time that this is in fact the case—indeed, Graham
74: conjectured this in [7] and offered $1000 for its proof.
75:      Various cases of this conjecture have been proved. In the original paper
76: [2] it is shown, by means of a product argument, that if X and Y are Ramsey
77: then so is X × Y . (Here if X ⊂ Rn and Y ⊂ Rm then we regard X × Y
78: as a subset of Rn+m .) In particular, it follows that any brick, meaning the
79: set of vertices of a cuboid in n dimensions, is Ramsey. Further progress
80: has been slow, with each new step requiring significant new ideas. Frankl
81: and Rödl [5] showed that every triangle is Ramsey, and later [6] that every
82: non-degenerate simplex is Ramsey. This left the next interesting case as the
83: regular pentagon, which was shown to be Ramsey by Křı́ž [11]. Křı́ž actually
84: showed that any finite set X which is acted on transitively by a soluble
85: group G of isometries is Ramsey and, slightly more generally, that G need
87: has at most two orbits. In particular, this implies that all regular polygons
88: are Ramsey and that the Platonic solids in 3 dimensions are Ramsey. In
89: addition, this result was used by Cantwell [1] to show that the 120-cell, the
90: largest regular polytope in 4 dimensions, is Ramsey. However, the conjecture
91: 
92:                                        2
93: 
94: itself is still wide open. Indeed, it is not even known whether or not every
95: cyclic quadrilateral is Ramsey.
96:     Our starting point in this paper is a feature that we have observed to
97: be common to all known proofs that particular sets are Ramsey. In each
98: case, the proof that the set is Ramsey proceeds by first embedding it in
99: a (finite) transitive set—a set whose symmetry group acts transitively—and
100: then making some clever combinatorial argument to show that this transitive
116: can differ greatly from paper to paper, and the transitive set can have a
117: much higher dimension than the original set, but we have noticed that the
118: transitivity is always present. Based on this, and some other facts (discussed
119: below), we are led to the following conjecture which asserts that transitivity
120: is the key property.
121: Conjecture A. A finite set X ⊂ Rn is Ramsey if and only if it is (congruent
122: to) a subset of a finite transitive set.
123:     In general, when we say that a set X is transitive we shall assume im-
124: plicitly that X is finite. For brevity, we shall say that a finite set in Rd is
125: subtransitive if it is congruent to a subset of a transitive set in some Rn . We
126: stress that this transitive set may have higher dimension than the original
127: subtransitive set.
128:     We believe that this is a very natural conjecture for various reasons. To
129: begin with, it turns out that there are several clean statements any of which
130: would imply that all transitive sets are Ramsey. Moreover, some of these
131: statements are purely combinatorial. In the other direction, the transitivity
132: of every Ramsey set would give a clear conceptual reason as to why every
133: Ramsey set must be spherical. Indeed, it is easy to see that the points of
134: 
135:                                        3
136: 
137: any transitive set all lie on the surface of the unique smallest closed ball
138: containing it.
139:      We remark that it is not clear a priori that Conjecture A is genuinely
140: different from the old conjecture: could it be that a finite set X is spherical
141: if and only if it is subtransitive? As we remark above, every subtransitive
142: set is spherical, but do there exist spherical sets that are not subtransitive?
143: We show that such sets do indeed exist. This result may be of independent
144: interest. One might hope that the proof of this result would give some insight
145: into showing the existence of a spherical set that is not Ramsey. However,
146: we do not see a way to make this work.
147:      The plan of the paper is as follows. In §2, we consider the ‘if’ direction
148: of our conjecture: ‘every subtransitive set is Ramsey’, or, equivalently, ‘ev-
149: ery transitive set is Ramsey’. Our first step is to remove the geometry, so
150: to say: we show that this direction of Conjecture A would follow from a
151: Hales-Jewett-type statement for groups (Conjecture C). Our next step is to
152: remove the group theory by showing that Conjecture C can be reformulated
153: as another equivalent Hales-Jewett-type statement which is purely combina-
154: torial (Conjecture E). This shows that our conjecture is, in a certain sense,
155: natural—if Conjecture E is true then it will provide a genuine combinatorial
156: reason why every transitive set must be Ramsey. In §3 we prove some of the
157: first non-trivial cases of Conjecture E. This is already enough to yield some
158: new examples of Ramsey sets. In §4, we show the existence of spherical sets
159: that are not subtransitive. More precisely, we show that, for any k ⩾ 16,
160: almost every cyclic k-gon is not subtransitive. We remark that our proof is
161: non-constructive: we have no explicit example of such a k-gon. Finally, in
162: §5, we briefly discuss the ‘only if’ direction of Conjecture A and give some
163: further problems.
164:      For a general overview of Ramsey Theory, see the book of Graham, Roth-
165: schild and Spencer [8]. We make use of van der Waerden’s theorem [14], and
166: certain formulations of our main conjecture have a similar flavour to the
167: Hales-Jewett theorem [9]; for both of these, see [8]. For further related re-
168: sults and problems, we refer the reader to the original sequence of papers [2],
169: [3] and [4] by Erdős, Graham, Montgomery, Rothschild, Spencer and Straus,
170: and to the later survey [7] of Graham. For previous work on subtransitive
171: sets, see Johnson [10].
172:      Our notation is standard. In particular, for natural numbers m, n with
173: m ≤ n we write [n] for the set {1, 2, . . . , n} and [m, n] for the set {m, m +
174: 1, . . . n}. For any set A, we write A(m) for the collection of subsets of A of
175: order m. If A is a set of integers and j is an integer, we write A + j for the
176: set {i + j : i ∈ A}. We write Sn to denote the symmetric group of all n!
179:                                       4
180: 
181: 2      Is every transitive set Ramsey?
182: We now discuss the ‘if’ direction of Conjecture A: how might we prove that
183: every transitive set is Ramsey?
184:     Refining the notion of Ramsey, we say that a set Y ⊂ Rd is k-Ramsey for
185: X if any k-colouring of Y yields a subset congruent to X. It follows from a
186: compactness argument that if X is Ramsey then, for each k, there is a finite
187: set Y such that Y is k-Ramsey for X (see [2] or [8]).
188:     As we remarked above, every set known to be Ramsey is proved so by
189: first embedding it in a finite transitive set X. In fact, all such proofs then
190: continue by colouring a large product X n , or, more precisely, a scaling λX n =
191: {λx : x ∈ X n }. (For X ⊂ Rm , we view X n as a subset of Rmn .) This leads
192: us to make the following stronger conjecture.
193: Conjecture B. Let X ⊂ Rm be a finite transitive set. Then, for any k,
194: there exists an n such that some scaling of X n is k-Ramsey for X.
195:      The ‘if’ direction of Conjecture A would, of course, follow immediately
196: from Conjecture B.
197:      We digress for a moment to comment on a related notion. In their initial
198: paper [2] on Euclidean Ramsey theory, Erdős, Graham, Montgomery, Roth-
199: schild, Spencer and Straus define a set X to be sphere-Ramsey if for any
200: positive integer k there exists a positive integer d and a positive real number
201: r such that whenever {x ∈ Rd+1 : kxk = r}, the d-dimensional sphere of
202: radius r, is k-coloured it contains a subset congruent to X. It is obvious that
203: any sphere-Ramsey set must be Ramsey. As we observed earlier, it is easy to
204: show that any transitive set is spherical. Hence Conjectures A and B would
205: together imply that a set is sphere-Ramsey if and only if it is Ramsey.
206:      Our aim in the remainder of this section will be to reformulate Conjecture
207: B as an equivalent purely combinatorial statement (Conjecture E). Our first
208: step is to ‘remove the geometry’ by recasting Conjecture B in terms of the
209: symmetry group of X.
210:      Let G be a group, n a positive integer and I ⊂ [n]. Suppose ~g =
211: (g1 , g2 , . . . , gn ) ∈ Gn and h ∈ G. We write ~g ×I h for the word (k1 , k2 , . . . , kn ) ∈
212: Gn where                                
213:                                            gi if i 6∈ I
214:                                    ki =                   .
215:                                           gi h if i ∈ I
216: As we shall see, Conjecture B is equivalent to the following conjecture.
217: Conjecture C. Let G be a finite group. Then for any positive integer k
218: there exist positive integers n and d such that whenever Gn is k-coloured
219: there exists a word ~g ∈ Gn and a set I ⊂ [n] with |I| = d such that the set
220: {~g ×I h : h ∈ G} is monochromatic.
221: 
222:                                               5
223: 
224:     The reader familiar with the Hales-Jewett theorem [9] will see some re-
225: semblance. Indeed, without the restriction ‘|I| = d’, Conjecture C would be
226: an easy consequence of the Hales-Jewett theorem.
227:     We remark that in Conjecture C we cannot insist that d = 1. More
228: generally, we cannot even insist that ~g be constant on I. Indeed, to see
229: this, 2-colour Gn by colouring a vector according to whether the number of
230: occurrences of the identity element e lies between 1 and d or between d + 1
231: and 2d (modulo 2d).
232:     We next show how Conjecture B may be deduced directly from Conjec-
233: ture C.

235: Proposition 2.1. Conjecture C implies Conjecture B
236: 
237: Proof. Assume that Conjecture C is true. Let X be a finite transitive set
238: and let k be a positive integer. Let G be the symmetry group of X. By
239: Conjecture C, we may choose n in such a way that whenever Gn is k-coloured
240: there exists a word ~g ∈ Gn and a set I ⊂ [n] with |I| = d such that the set
241: {~g ×I h : h ∈ G} is monochromatic.
242:     Suppose X n is k-coloured. We induce a k-colouring of Gn by picking
243: x ∈ X and giving (g1 , g2 , . . . , gn ) the colour of (g1 (x), g2 (x), . . . , gn (x)). Now
244: choose I ⊂ [n] with |I| = d and ~g ∈ Gn such that the set {~g ×I h : h ∈ G}
245: is monochromatic. For notational convenience, assume wlog that I = [d], so
246: that the set

259: Z are isometric. Hence √1m X n is k-Ramsey for X.
260:     Conjectures B and C are in fact equivalent, but we have no simple and
261: direct way of deducing Conjecture C from Conjecture B. In the case of a
262: group G which acts as the symmetry group of some transitive set of order |G|,
263: Conjecture C may be deduced easily from Conjecture B. But, for example,
264: the cyclic group C3 does not act as the symmetry group of any 3-point set.
265: The proof of this direction of the equivalence must wait until the end of the
266: current section.

271:        Having successfully removed the geometry, our next task is to ‘remove
272: the groups’. One may think of Conjecture C as saying that the varying parts
273: of our m words (m = |G|) contain the group table of G, possibly with some
274: columns omitted and some columns repeated. That is, we obtain the m
275: rows of some Latin-square-type pattern. Instead of asking merely for these
276: m rows, we could instead demand all m! permutations of the elements of
277: G. This now gives us a purely combinatorial statement—a ‘fixed-block-size’
278: Hales-Jewett-type conjecture.
279:        We first need a preliminary definition. We wish to consider a collection of
280: words of the following form: we fix m blocks, make our words the same as each
281: other outside these blocks, and take each of the m! possible arrangements of
282: 1, 2, . . . , m amongst the blocks. Formally, a block permutation set in [m]n is
283: a set B formed in the following way. First, select pairwise
284:                                                       Sm      disjoint subsets I1 ,
285: . . . , Im ⊂ [n] and elements ai ∈ [m] for each i 6∈ j=1 Ij . For each π ∈ Sm ,
286: define aπ ∈ [m]n by

297: the sets I1 , I2 , . . . , Im as blocks.
298:    We remark that a block permutation set need not contain precisely m!
299: elements: some of the blocks could be empty.
300: Conjecture D. Let m and k be positive integers. Then there exist positive
301: integers n and d such that whenever [m]n is k-coloured it contains a block
302: permutation set of degree d.
303:     Again, note that if the degree condition were omitted then this would
304: follow easily from the Hales-Jewett theorem. Indeed, any block permutation
305: set in [m]n is contained in some m-dimensional combinatorial subspace (m-
306: parameter set). Furthermore, as with Conjecture C, we cannot require each
307: block to have size 1.
308:     It is clear that Conjecture C follows from Conjecture D. While Conjecture
309: D appears much stronger, it is in fact equivalent to Conjecture C, as we now
310: show. (In fact, this follows from later results; we include it here because the
311: proof is concise and direct.)
312: Proposition 2.2. Conjectures C and D are equivalent.
313: Proof. For the non-trivial direction of the implication, assume Conjecture
314: C is true, and let m and k be positive integers. We apply Conjecture C

334:     We now give a further equivalent formulation of this conjecture which we
335: hope might be more amenable to proof.
336:     Conjecture D asks for a collection of words of a certain type all of the
337: same colour. We may think of this collection as being represented by the
338: ‘pattern’ 12 . . . m. More generally, we could consider an arbitrary pattern.
339: For example, to realise the pattern 11223 we would demand five blocks, and a
340: word for each way of assigning the symbol 1 to two of the blocks, the symbol
341: 2 to another two of the blocks and the symbol 3 to the remaining block.
342: So in this case we would have a total of 30 words (if all of the blocks were
343: non-empty).

345: τ ∈ [m]ℓ for some ℓ.
346:     Next, we define a block set with template τ . The reader should bear in
369:                   P
370:     As before, if ℓj=1 |Ij | = d then we say that B is of degree d, and we may
371: refer to the sets I1 , I2 , . . . , Iℓ as blocks.
372:     We are now ready to state the conjecture.
373: Conjecture E. Let m and k be positive integers and let τ be a template over
374: [m]. Then there exist positive integers n and d such that whenever [m]n is
375: k-coloured it contains a monochromatic block set of degree d with template τ .
376:     It is easy to see that Conjectures D and E are equivalent. Conjecture D
377: is simply the special case of Conjecture E for the template 12 . . . m. In the
378: other direction, Conjecture E for the template τ1 . . . τℓ on alphabet [m] follows
379: immediately from Conjecture D on alphabet [ℓ]. Indeed, given a colouring of
380: [m]n we use the map (x1 , . . . , xn ) 7→ (τx1 , . . . , τxn ) to induce a colouring of
381: [ℓ]n and then apply Conjecture D.
382:     What can we say about the initial cases of Conjecture E? The case m = 1
383: is trivial. In the next case, m = 2, the conjecture is true for all templates by
384: an easy application of Ramsey’s theorem, as we shall explain in §3. We shall
385: then prove the first non-trivial cases: templates of the form 1 . . . 12 . . . 23.
386:     It turns out that, in all of these cases, the block set we produce is uniform:
387: that is, all of its blocks have the same size. This suggests the following
388: conjecture, which perhaps appears more natural.
389: Conjecture F. Let m and k be positive integers and let τ be a template over
390: [m]. Then there exist positive integers n and d such that whenever [m]n is
391: k-coloured it contains a monochromatic uniform block set of degree d with
392: template τ .
393:     At first sight, Conjecture F may appear rather stronger than Conjec-
394: ture E—we have imposed an additional constraint on the monochromatic
395: block set that we seek. However, they do in fact turn out to be equivalent.
396: We do not see how to deduce Conjecture F directly from Conjecture E, but
397: instead go via the geometric Conjecture B.
398:     To prove that all of the conjectures of this section (Conjectures B–F) are
399: equivalent, it now suffices to show that we may deduce Conjecture F from
400: Conjecture B. So, how might we use Conjecture B to deduce Conjecture F
401: for the template 1 . . . m? One approach would be to ‘embed’ the template
402: into Rm as follows.
403:     Suppose α1 , α2 , . . . , αm are real numbers. Let X be the set in Rm of all
404: permutations of the vector (α1 , α2 , . . . , αm ) and let Y = {α1 , α2 , . . . , αm }nm .
405: Note that X n ⊂ Y . We shall think of Y as the image of [m]nm in the obvious
406: way.
407:     Certainly X is transitive (Sm acts on it), so by Conjecture B we know
408: that (provided n is sufficiently large) whenever Y is k-coloured it contains
409: 
410:                                             9
411: 
412: a monochromatic set congruent to a fixed scaling sX of X. One way such
413: a set could occur is as the image of an s2 -uniform block set, but of course
414: there may be many other ways as well.
415:     The heart of the proof is to ensure that the only subsets of Y congruent
416: to sX are the images of s2 -uniform block sets.
417: Lemma 2.3. Let α1 , α2 , . . . , αm be algebraically independent real numbers

675: 4     Not all spherical sets are subtransitive
676: In this section, we show that our conjecture is genuinely different from the
677: old conjecture [7], by showing that there exists a finite spherical set that is
678: not subtransitive. Specifically, we show that if k ⩾ 16 then there exists a
679: cyclic k-gon that is not subtransitive.
680:     One natural approach is to aim for a non-constructive proof showing that
681: almost no cyclic k-gon is subtransitive. However, the space of cyclic k-gons
682: has k degrees of freedom, whereas the space of (non-isometric) orbits of a
683: fixed group of isometries of Rn can have many more. This is the main obstacle
684: that we have to overcome.
685:     For convenience, we consider labelled, oriented k-gons, i. e. we label the
686: vertices 1, 2, . . . , k in clockwise order and consider two the same if there is an
687: isometry between them preserving the labels. Now suppose that x1 . . . xk is
789: 
790:     We also find it unlikely that it is necessary to go as far as 16-gons:
791: 
792: Conjecture I. There exists a cyclic quadrilateral that is not subtransitive.
793: 
794:     Indeed, we believe that almost no cyclic quadrilateral should be subtran-
795: sitive.
796:     We remark that it is easy to check that all trapezia are subtransitive.
797: In fact, Křı́ž [12] showed that all trapezia are Ramsey. This may also be
798: deduced from his general result in [11].
799: 
800: 
801: 5     Are Ramsey sets subtransitive?
802: For this direction of our conjecture, we have no results at all—so in this
803: section we only mention a few heuristic ideas.
804:      Given a Ramsey set X, why might there be a transitive set containing
805: it? There are certainly some ‘structured’ sets containing X—namely the sets
806: that are k-Ramsey (for some k) for X. Of course, it is impossible that every
807: such set is transitive, as we may always add points to a set that is k-Ramsey
808: for X to destroy any symmetry that is present. So one should focus on the
809: minimal k-Ramsey sets for X.
810:      To fix our ideas, let us consider the simplest possible case, when X is the
811: set {0, 1}. What are the minimal 2-Ramsey sets for X? For a finite set S
812: in Rn , define the graph of S to be the graph on vertex-set S in which we
847:      Finally, we consider an algorithmic question. It is easy to see that one
848: can determine in finite time whether or not a given set is spherical. However,
849: it is not clear that it is possible to determine in finite time whether or not a
850: given set is subtransitive. So if our conjecture were true, it would leave open
851: the problem of finding an algorithm to determine whether or not a given set
852: is Ramsey. So we ask:
853: 
854: Problem K. Is there an algorithm for testing in finite time whether or not
855: a given set is subtransitive?

27:                                                                            1. Introduction
28:                                              In this paper we show how the Method of Rotations and Tight Frames
29:                                           developed in our earlier paper for plane domains [20] can be extended to
30:                                           yield higher dimensional bounds on sums of eigenvalues of the Laplacian.
31:                                           The transition to higher dimensions requires a broader algebraic context,
76: fill the lowest n quantum states under the Pauli exclusion principle. The
77: second is mathematical: summability methods provide a tools for studying
78: high eigenvalues, which are difficult to study individually. An example of the
79: gains possible by summation is that while Pólya’s conjecture (claiming the
80: Weyl asymptotic is a lower bound for each Dirichlet eigenvalue) is still open,
81: Li and Yau [21] have shown that sums of eigenvalues are indeed bounded
82: below by the analogous Weyl asymptotic.
83:     Notice our work in this paper is geometrically sharp, with an extremal
84: domain existing for each fixed n. The Li–Yau inequality is not geometrically
85: sharp, but is asymptotically sharp — equality holds for each domain in the
86: limit as n → ∞. Asymptotically sharp upper bounds in the Neumann
87: case were obtained by Kröger [19], and sums of functions of eigenvalues
88: were studied recently by Frank, Geisinger, Harrell, Hermi, Laptev, Loss and
89: Weidl ([7, 8, 11, 14] and references therein).
241: pose), because the transpose changes neither the volume nor the second
242: moment of mass of the domain (see Lemma 5.2 and note that T −1 and its
243: transpose have the same Hilbert–Schmidt norm). We have chosen to state
244: the “transposed” version of the corollary because it leads to a conjecture for
245: polar duals, in Section 4.
246:    In two dimensions, Corollary 3.3 reduces to saying that (λ1 +· · ·+λn )A3 /I
247: is maximal for the equilateral triangle among triangles (respectively: square
248: among parallelograms, disk among ellipses), as one sees by taking d = 2,
249: V = A, and using an identity connecting the moments of inertia of T (D) and
250: T −1 (D) in two dimensions [20, Lemma 5.4]. This two dimensional version
251: of Corollary 3.3 appeared in our earlier paper [20, Corollary 3.2].
252:    An obvious generalization of this two dimensional estimate would be to
253: maximize (λ1 + · · · + λn )V 1+4/d /I, but this functional is unbounded even
254: on the family of rectangular boxes in three dimensions, as the next example

106: The proof of the 3k − 3 theorem above in [Fr1] was not short while the proof of the 3k − 2
107: theorem was omitted there because, commented by Freiman, it was too tedious2 . There
108: has been no further accurate characterization, until now, of the structure of A when, for
109: example, |2A| = 3k − 1. In fact, Freiman made the following conjecture a few years ago in
110: [Fr2].
111: 
112: Conjecture 1.1 (G. A. Freiman) There exists a natural number K such that for any
113: finite set of natural numbers A with |A| = k > K and |2A| = 3k − 3 + b for 0 ⩽ b < 31 k − 2,
114: A is either a subset of an a.p. of length at most 2k − 1 + 2b or a subset of a b.p. of length
115: at most k + b.
116: 
117:     Note that Conjecture 1.1 is clearly false if b = 31 k − 2 as shown in the following easy
118: example.
119: 
120: Example 1.2 Suppose k = 3a and c > 2k. Let A = [0, a − 1] ∪ [c, c + a − 1] ∪ [2c, 2c + a − 1].
121: Then |A| = k and |2A| = 3|A| − 3 + 31 k − 2. But A is neither a subset of an a.p. of length
122: 2k − 1 + 2( 13 k − 2) nor a subset of a b.p. of length k + ( 31 k − 2).
123: 
124:     It is easy to prove Freiman’s conjecture if one adds an extra condition that the set A is
125: a subset of a b.p. We prove this in Theorem 1.3 as a simple consequence of Theorem A.3.
126:     Let A and B be two subsets of two torsion–free groups, respectively. A bijection φ :
127: A 7→ B is called a F2 -isomorphism3 if for all a1 , a2 , a3 , a4 ∈ A, a1 + a2 = a3 + a4 if and only
128: if φ(a1 ) + φ(a2 ) = φ(a3 ) + φ(a4 ). A set

269:     We prove Theorem 1.7 in the next several sections. The proof is done in two steps. In
270: the first step we deal with the case when A ⊆ [0, H] contains significantly less than half of
271: the elements in [0, H]. In the second step we deal with the case when A ⊆ [0, H] contains
272: roughly half of the elements in [0, H]. The main theorem in each step is preceded by a list
273: of lemmas, which prove the theorem under various circumstances. Before these two steps
274: we present a list of general lemmas. For convenience we include some existing theorems
275: in Appendix for quick references. In this paper, theorems, lemmas, cases, and claims are
276: numbered in such a way that the reader should be able to see how they are nested.

2880: 6    Comments and a Conjecture
2881: 
2882: The reader might notice that the proof of the case when |A| ≺ 12 H is much more “nonstan-
2883: dard” than the proof of the case when |A| ∼ 12 H, which is combinatorial. However, the
2884: proof of the latter is significantly simplified after the possibility of |A| ≺ 21 H is eliminated.
2885:     After reading all the proofs above, the reader should be able to see the crucial role that
2886: Lemma 2.2 plays. In order to violate the condition |2A| ∼ 3|A| one needs only to find a
2887: small segment A[a, b] of the set A, which already violates (2A)(2a, 2b) ∼ 3A(a, b), as long
2888: as the rest of the A at each side of the segment is not too dense and is not a subset of
2889: an a.p. of difference > 1 (see the condition of Lemma 2.3). So if A ∩ U does not have
2890: expected structural properties such as dU (A) > 12 , dU (A) = 0, A ∩ U is a subset of an a.p.
2902: of a finite interval cannot be closed under usual addition. This indicates that the use of
2903: nonstandard analysis in this paper is non-trivial.
2904:     Although Theorem 1.4 is a significant advancement of the current results, it confirms
2905: only a weak version of Conjecture 1.1. It is interesting to see whether the ideas from
2906: nonstandard analysis can play a major role in the ultimate solution of Conjecture 1.1. Many
2907: lemmas including Lemma 2.2 in this paper may be generalized. If these generalizations are
2908: achieved, then one can generalize Theorem 1.4 by allowing |2A| ⩽ α < 10
2909:                                                                       3 |A|. I would like
2910: to state that as a conjecture. The conjecture stated below should be much easier to prove
2911: than proving Conjecture 1.1. However, the solution of the following conjecture is useful for
2912: improving Corollary 5.1 and could be the last stepping stone to the ultimate solution of
2913: Conjecture 1.1.
2914: 
2915: Conjecture 6.1 For any real number α with 3 < α < 3 + 13 there exists a K ∈ N such that
2916: for every finite set A ⊆ N with |A| > K, if 3|A| − 3 ⩽ |2A| = 3|A| − 3 + b ⩽ α|A|, then A
2917: is either a subset of an a.p. of length at most 2|A| − 1 + 2b or a subset of a b.p. of length
2918: at most |A| + b.

105: directed subforests. This construction, suggested to us by R. Stanley, is especially
106: important in the case of a complete double directed graph Gn , where it leads to
107: studying certain representations of the symmetric group and relates (via Stanley-
108: Reisner correspondence) to an interesting quotient ring. In the fourth chapter we
109: study complexes ∆(G) and associated quotient complexes.

3038:                                                                               2
3039: Following [SuW97], we give a different interpretation of the numbers β̃i (ΣSλ , Q),
3040: for general λ. First, let us observe that the symmetric group Sn acts on Cn by
3041: permuting the coordinates, so we can consider the space Cn /Sn endowed with
3531: easy to see that there is a unique vertex in XΛ,µ labeled γk (µ) and that K is a cone
3532: with an apex in this vertex. 
