3534: 3.5 On a conjecture of Sundaram and Welker                                                  73
3535: 
3536: 
3537:              3.5    O N A CONJECTURE OF S UNDARAM AND W ELKER
3538: 
3539: 3.5.1 A counterexample to the general conjecture
3540: 
3541: The original formulation of Conjecture 3.1.3 in [SuW97] was
3542: 
3543: Conjecture 3.5.1 [SuW97, Conjectures 4.12 and 4.13]. Let λ and µ be different
3544: set partitions, such that λ ⊢ µ. Let π ∈ Πλ be a partition of type µ. Then the mul-
3545: tiplicity of the trivial representation in the Stab (π)-module He ∗ (∆(Πλ (0̂, π)), Q)
3546: is 0.
3547:     In our terms Conjecture 3.5.1 is equivalent to
3548: Conjecture 3.5.2 For λ ⊢ µ, λ 6= µ, the space Xλ,µ is Q-acyclic.
3549:    We shall give an example when Xλ,µ is not even connected. It turns out that if
3550: one is only interested in counting the number of connected components of Xλ,µ ,
3551: then there is a simpler poset model which we now proceed to describe.
3552: 
3553: Definition 3.5.3 Assume λ ⊢ µ ⊢ n, λ 6= µ. The (λ, µ)-forests of rank 0 can
3554: be partially ordered as follows: (T1 , η1 ) ≺ (T2 , η2 ) if there exists a (λ, µ)-forest
3555: (T, η) of rank 1 such that (T1 , η1 ) = (T 1 , η 1 ) and (T2 , η2 ) = (T 0 , η 0 ). We call the
3556: obtained poset Pλ,µ .
3557: 
3558: In other words, elements of Pλ,µ are number partitions τ 6= µ such that λ ⊢
3578: 74                     Spaces of Complex Monic Polynomials and Complexes of Forests
3579: 
3580: 
3581: Counterexample. For n = 23, λ = (7, 6, 4, 3, 2, 1), µ = (10, 8, 5), Xλ,µ is
3582: disconnected. Pλ,µ is shown on the Figure 3.4. Clearly ∆(Pλ,µ ) is not connected,
3583: hence, by the Proposition 3.5.4, neither is Xλ,µ , which disproves Conjecture 3.5.1.
3584:

3586: 
3587: 
3588: 
3589:        (5)(6,2)(7,3)   (4,1)(8)(7,3)     (4,1)(6,2)(10)   (5)(7,1)(6,4)    (3,2)(8)(6,4)    (3,2)(7,1)(10)

3597: Remark 3.5.5 In the counterexample above, one can actually verify that Xλ,µ =
3598: ∆(Pλ,µ ). However, we choose to use posets Pλ,µ for two reasons:
3599: 
3600:      1. it is easier to produce series of counterexamples to Sundaram-Welker con-
3601:         jecture using ∆(Pλ,µ ) rather than Xλ,µ ;
3602: 
3603:      2. we feel that posets Pλ,µ are of independent interest, since they are in a cer-
3604:         tain sense the “naive” quotient of Πλ (0̂, π) by Stab (π).
3605: 
3606:      We believe that, in general, connected components of Xλ,µ may be not acyclic.

3611: Definition 3.5.6 We say that a number partition λ = (λ1 , . . . , λt ) is generic (also
3612: called freePof resonances
3613:                       P in [ShW98], having no equal sub-sums in [Ko97]) if
3614: whenever i∈I λi = j∈J λj , for some I, J ⊆ [t], we have {λi }i∈I = {λj }j∈J
3615: as multisets.
3616: 
3617:      For example λ = (k m ) is generic.

16:                                                     of the generalized associahedron for W. The rays of the c-Cambrian fan are
17:                                                     generated by certain vectors in the W -orbit of the fundamental weights, while
18:                                                     the rays of the c-cluster fan are generated by certain roots. We show (subject
19:                                                     to a conjecture of Fomin and Zelevinsky) that in the crystallographic case,
20:                                                     expressing a ray of the c-Cambrian fan in the basis of the fundamental weights
21:                                                     yields the g-vectors defined by Fomin and Zelevinsky. Using this result, we
22:                                                     are able to establish (subject to the same conjecture) Fomin and Zelevinsky’s
23:                                                     sign coherence conjecture for acyclic seeds in cluster algebras of finite type.
24:                                                     For particular (“bipartite”) choices of c, we show that the c-Cambrian fan is
25:                                                     linearly isomorphic to the c-cluster fan. We characterize, in terms of the com-
26:                                                     binatorics of clusters, the partial order induced, via the map clc , on c-clusters
27:                                                     by the c-Cambrian lattice. Finally, we give a simple bijection from c-clusters
28:                                                     to c-noncrossing partitions that respects the refined (Narayana) enumeration.

123: phism, combined with the close structural relationship which exists between the
124: c-Cambrian lattice and the c-Cambrian fan (see Section 5), implies that the Hasse
125: diagram of the c-Cambrian lattice is combinatorially isomorphic to the 1-skeleton of
126: the generalized associahedron (Corollary 8.1). This confirms [18, Conjecture 1.2.a].
127:     The c-Cambrian lattice induces a partial order (the c-cluster lattice) on c-clusters
128: via the map clc . We characterize this partial order in terms of the combinatorics
129: of clusters (Theorem 8.4), generalizing and proving the second statement of [18,
130: Conjecture 1.4]. The c-cluster lattice inherits many useful properties from the
131: c-Cambrian lattice (see Corollary 8.5), including the property that any linear ex-
132: tension of the c-cluster poset is a shelling of the c-cluster fan. As a consequence
133: of this shelling property we obtain, in Section 8, a bijective proof of the fact that
134: the k th entry of the h-vector of the c-cluster fan coincides with the number of c-
135: noncrossing partitions of rank k. (This number is called the k th Narayana number
136: associated to W.)
137:     Theorem 1.1 also leads to the proof of the first statement of [18, Conjecture 1.4],
138: namely that the c-Cambrian fan is linearly isomorphic to the c-cluster fan when c
139: is chosen to be a bipartite Coxeter element (Theorem 9.1). Using this linear iso-
140: morphism and a “twisted” version of the c-cluster poset, we give, in Section 11, a
141: purely geometric description of a bijection between c-clusters and c-noncrossing par-
142: titions. Another connection between noncrossing partitions and clusters has arisen
143: recently. Brady and Watt [6] construct a simplicial fan associated to c-noncrossing
144: partitions (for bipartite c) and extend their construction to produce the c-cluster
145: fan. Athanasiadis, Brady, McCammond and Watt [1] use the construction of [6] to
146: give a bijection between clusters and noncrossing partitions. Their proof uses no
147: type by type arguments and provides a different bijective proof that the k th entry
169: two vectors in Zn : the denominator vector and the g-vector of x with respect to t.
170: It is shown in [9] and [7] that, when t = tc the denominator vector is found by
171: expressing the corresponding root in the basis of simple roots. We show, assuming
172: [10, Conjecture 7.12], that the g-vector is given by expressing the corresponding
173: ray of the c-Cambrian fan in the basis of fundamental weights. Using this result
174: (and still assuming [10, Conjecture 7.12]), we establish Fomin and Zelevinsky’s sign
175: coherence conjecture ([10, Conjecture 6.13]) for t of the form tc .

177:                           2. Main results and examples
178:    In this section we define Cambrian fans and cluster fans in just enough depth
179: to explain the main results precisely and illustrate them with a running example.
180: More substantial examples are found in later sections. We assume familiarity with
181: the most basic definitions surrounding Coxeter groups.
182:    Let W be a finite Coxeter group of rank n with simple generators S. Recall
183: that a reduced word for w ∈ W is an expression for w in the generators S which is
184: minimal in length among all such expressions for w. Let s1 s2 · · · sn be a sequence
185: consisting of each element of S exactly once, so that the product of the sequence is

1381: the main theorem of this section, which is a generalization of the second statement
1382: of [18, Conjecture 1.4].
1383: Theorem 8.4. The map clc is an isomorphism of lattices from the c-Cambrian
1384: lattice W/Θc to the c-cluster lattice.

1389:    (i) Clustc is a lattice.

130: Theorem 1.1 is typically only piecewise linear. However, we show that for any W ,
131: there exists a special “bipartite” choice of c such that the c-Cambrian fan and the
132: c-cluster fan are linearly isomorphic. This result (Theorem 9.1) verifies the first
133: statement of [27, Conjecture 1.4].
134:    Theorem 1.1 shows that c-sortable elements are not simply in bijection with
135: c-clusters, but define the same underlying combinatorial structure. This is a par-
136: ticularly surprising result as the Cambrian fan and cluster fan are defined in very
137: different ways: the Cambrian fan is defined by removing walls of the fan defined by
138: the reflecting hyperplanes while the cluster fan is defined by choosing certain rays,
139: all of which are normal to reflecting hyperplanes, and specifying which rays lie in
140: common cones. Moreover, the Cambrian fan contributes combinatorial structure
141: which is not present in the cluster fan, including a poset (in fact lattice) structure
142: which interacts well with the fan structure, as well as a notion of projection to
143: standard parabolic subgroups.

163: diagram of the c-Cambrian lattice is combinatorially isomorphic to the 1-skeleton of
164: the generalized associahedron (Corollary 8.1). This confirms [27, Conjecture 1.2.a].
165:    The c-Cambrian lattice induces a partial order (the c-cluster lattice) on c-clusters
166: via the map clc . We characterize this partial order in terms of the combinatorics
167: of clusters (Theorem 8.4), generalizing and proving the second statement of [27,

170: tension of the c-cluster poset is a shelling of the c-cluster fan. As a consequence

175:    In Section 11 we give a purely geometric description (Theorem 11.4) of a bijection
176: between c-clusters and c-noncrossing partitions, in the case where c is a bipartite
177: Coxeter element. This result draws on a “twisted” version of the c-cluster poset as
178: well as the linear isomorphism, mentioned above, between the c-Cambrian fan and
186: partitions of rank k. The bijection of [1] incorporates elements which are similar in
187: appearance to the constructions of the present paper (see Remark 11.5), but many
188: details of the relation between the two theories remain unclear.
189:    The results described above resolve all conjectures from [27] except for Conjec-
190: ture 1.1. This last result has been established by Hohlweg and Lange [17] for types
191: A and B and will be proven for all types in a future paper by Hohlweg, Lange and
192: Thomas [18].
193:    The relationship between the c-Cambrian fan and the c-cluster fan has several
194: consequences for the theory of cluster algebras, which we describe in more detail in
195: Section 10. When W admits a crystallographic root system Φ, Fomin and Zelevin-
196: sky associate a cluster algebra Alg(Φ) to W. This is a commutative algebra with
197: certain specified elements, called cluster variables, and certain specified subsets of
198: these variables, ordinarily called clusters. We will call these subsets of variables
199: algebraic clusters, to distinguish them from the combinatorially defined clusters

23:                                                    G.
24:                                                        Let G be a 2-connected K1,5 -free 3-vertex-critical graph. For any vertex
25:                                                    v ∈ V (G), we show that G − v has a perfect matching (except two graphs),
26:                                                    which is a conjecture posed by Ananchuen and Plummer [2].
27:                                                    Key words: matching, factor-critical, dominating set, 3-vertex-critical graphs
28:                                                    AMS 2000 Subject Classifications: 05C69, 05C70.
29: 
30: 
31: 
32:                                              1    Introduction
33: 
34:                                              Let G be a finite simple graph with vertex set V (G) and edge set E(G). A vertex
35:                                              subset S of G is a dominating set of G if every vertex of G either belongs to S or is
36:                                              adjacent to a vertex of S. The minimum size of such a set is called the dominating
64: Plummer [1, 2] began to study matchings in 3-vertex-critical graphs. They showed
65: that a K1,5 -free 3-vertex-critical graph of even order has a perfect matching (see
66: [1]) and a K1,4 -free 3-vertex-critical graph of odd order is factor-critical (see [2]).
67: Furthermore, they posed the following conjecture.
68: 
69: Conjecture 1. If G is a K1,5 -free 3-vertex-critical 2-connected graph of odd order
70: with δ(G) ≥ 3, then G is factor-critical.
71: 
72:     In this paper, we show that the conjecture holds for almost all graphs and there
73: are only two counterexamples.
74: 
75: 
76:     If v ∈ V (G), we denote by Dv , a minimum dominating set of G − v. The following
77: facts about Dv follow immediately from the definition of 3-vertex-criticality and we
78: shall use it frequently in the proof of the main theorem.
79: Facts: If G is 3-vertex-critical, then the followings hold:
80: 
81:  (1) For every vertex v of G, |Dv | = 2.
82:  (2) If Dv = {x, y}, then x and y are not adjacent to v.
83:  (3) For every pair of distinct vertices v and w, Dv 6= Dw .
118:     Before giving our main result, we note that the graphs G1 and G2 in Figure 1 are
119: K1,5 -free 3-vertex-critical 2-connected graph of order 11 with δ(G) = 3, but are not
120: factor-critical, since Gi − vi has no perfect matching for i = 1, 2. We shall show that
121: these two graphs are the only two counterexamples for Conjecture 1.
122:

281: However, there are only four such graphs under the isomorphism, see Figure 1 and
282: Figure 2. It is easy to see that G3 and G4 are not 3-vertex-critical, since |Dvi | > 2 in
283: Gi for i = 3, 4. Therefore, G1 and G2 are two counterexamples to Conjecture 1.
284:    Case 5. k ≥ 6.
285:    Claim 5. For every vertex x ∈ V (G), Dx ⊆ S.
286:    Suppose that Dx * S for some x ∈ V (G). Clearly, Dx ∩ S 6= ∅. Let Dx = {y, z},
287: where y ∈ S and z ∈ V (G) − S. Since t ≥ 7, y must dominate at least 5 odd
288: components of G − S, a contradiction to K1,5 -freeness.
289:     Let w be any vertex in S, then Dw ⊆ S by Claim 5. Since G is K1,5 -free, each
290: vertex of Dw can dominate at most 4 components of G − S, which implies that the
291: number of components of G − S is at most 8 or t ≤ 8. That is, 6 ≤ k ≤ 7.
292:     Let Si ⊆ S be the set of vertices in S which are adjacent to some vertex in Ci for
293: i = 1, 2, . . . , t, and let d = min{|Si |}. Without loss of generality, assume that |S1 | = d.
